"CAPITAL" CELAT.

"Professors," said Lord Randolph Churchill at Bradford, only talk nonsense when they talk in politics. This is bard on those henchmen of the Unionist cause in England, Professor Fronde. Pro- fessor Tyndall, and Professor Dicey, and those pro- minent Irish Unionist -orators, Professor Mahaffy, Professor Dowden, and Professor Traill, to say nothing of the eminent Unionist leader, the Right Hon. ex-Professor Leonard Courtney, M.P., and the Tory member for Cambridge, Professor Jebb.

Serious apprehension is felt at the state of health of the little Queen Wilhelmina of the Netherlands. She is only thirteen years old, but she is consump- tive, and has undergone a change for the worse. Several prominent physicions have, writes a corres- pondent, been called in, and the result of their deliberations, which are kept as secret as possible, is said to be a grave opinion as to her recovery. The young Queen is rather pretty, with a somewhat more thoughtful face than girls of her age gener- ally possess. -- The Rev. Charles Pritchard, D.D., Savilian Pro- fessor of Astronomy at Oxford, who died on Sunday was one of the most distinguished scientists of the day. lie was educated at St. John's College, Cain- bridge, of which college he was until his death an honorary fellow. Mr. Pritchard was also a fellow of New College, Oxford, and a Fellow of the, Royal Society. He was Hilleeftll Lecturer at Cambridge in 1867, and had filled the post of President of the Royal Astronomical Society. The late professor was very popular among all his pupils at Oxford, and his death will create a void which will be very hard to fill. Mark Twain has just left America, where he has been for the past month,for Genoa,perhaps to make some further inquiries respecting the bust and handwriting of Columbus, concerning which it will be remembered there was some excellent fooling in **The Innocents Abroad." Mr. Clemens has been unlucky of late. He was seized by "la grippe" in Germany and has been suffering from it during the whole time of his stay in America. It is no secret that the famous humorist's eatly experiences as a compositor have aiways led him to pay a great deal of attention to matters typograpical, and that at present he is greatly interested in more senses than one in a new type-setting machine Which like all the others "threatens to revolutionise the whole art of printing." Mr. Lithouchere is not the first member of the Liberal party who has been caused annoyance by feeble attempts to imitate Theodore hook's colossal Berners-et., hoax. Precisely the same trick was played off upon Mr. Caine some three years ago; and in this case also the cloven hoof was clearly re- vealed ()Wing to the simultaneous arrival by post of filthy anuonymous letters. Sir Wilfred Lawson has also been under the necessity of refusing to ac- cept goods on the ground that they were not ordered. As the goods ordered in the name of Mr. Caine and Sir Wilfred Lawson usually took the shape of barrels of beer and cases of wine, the sup- position was that the forger of their names was one and the same person; probably the culprit thinks by this time his former exploits have been forgotten.

"What do you come here for ? " said the knight of the hammer in despair to his dumb audience at • Tokenhouse-yard on Monday, when he offered for sale a landed estate in the country, at the "low upset price of £3,800." Nor for the first time, or the twenty-first time, since the commencement of the season in the big city premises, was no bid forthcoming. The fact is these " low upset prices" are a farce; they are just as high as they were when there was a" boom in agricultural land. Nothing apparently, will bring the landowners to take a sensible view of the situation. The public can afford te watch and wait. What they are refused at auction to-day they know they will get by pri- _ vats contract in a week or two at a considerable reduction. Members of the Tory party who have been lax in their attendance at st. Stephen's since the com- mencement of the committee sage of the Home Rale Bill were in evidence on Monday night, and the lobby representative of the " Times," who is genar.illy understood to be responsible for the gib- ing idea, had a bad time. Admiral Field was especially anxious to vent his spleen, and not with- out-reason ;-for he is one of the few absentees who have reasonable excuses to offer for their apparent neglect." I must have somebody's blood,"said the old pea-dog; and just at that moment he ran up against the "Times" man. But it turned out that, after all, his,chief grievance was against the division clerks who had credited him with one vote less than he tad actually recorded.

Baron Sehroeder who is one of the chief exhibi-

tors at the Royal HorticulAiral Bociety's show in • the Temple Gardens, is one of the foremost orchid lovers in the country. At his charming place called The Dell, which is on the outskirts of Windsor Forest, and not very far from Holloway College, be has some generous conservatories, upon whose orchidaceous contents he must have spent .£40,000 at least. Not long ago he parted with a unique variety ot cypripedium for .£36s,and is never afraid of paying high prices for the particular species which he fancies most. His grounds are enclosed by a green hedge so perfectly cut that in the dis- tance it presents the appearance of a stone wall painted green. The orchid-houses are, of course, not open to the inspection of the general public.

CoMilani 'Hall; Bury St. Edmunds, which Was withdrawn at auttion on Tuesday at Tokenhouse- yark iS a perfect .fpecimen of a ooanty house on a" ansall twale of tint pl-riod of Queen Elizabeth, with • historical associations of a marked and interesting character. It was in the sixteenth century the pro- perty of the Rookwood family, a member of which belonged to the Guy Fawkes conspiracy. It contains a pretty chapel, with priests' hiding places. There has been no intermediate owner since the Rook- woods, whose heiress married a Gage, who, except the present owner,was the last occupier. The man- sion has the usual flrm and elevation of true

Elizabethan houses. The park of about one hundred acres iN wtll adorned with lofty elms and oaks, and the partridge shooting is excellent.

The other day the Queen of Belgians, under cir- cumstances of great interest, gave a fresh proof of her brilliant capacity iv+ a harpist. Queen :Marie Henrietta is passionately fond of music, and is a fine pianist, as well as a finished executant on the harp. She has composed an opera" Wanda," which deals with the potency of love, and of which a court performance has been given. It is on these grounds a pity that the king himself should abom- inate music in every form, and whenever the piano Is opened, after dinner, he Incontinently vanishes out of hearing. There is an excellent collection of M.S. music at the palace, but it is stowed away in an upper chamber, and much of it has never been played at all. Some of this is in the loveliest call- graphy imaginable, and the mere copying of it occupied the music-writer many months.

Mr. Henry M'Caltnont, the owner of Isinglass was not only the winner of the Derby, on Wednes- day, but bas come into four millions of money. He succeeded to his uncle's fortune, which was not to

come into his possession until he had attained the age of 32. an event which happened on Wednesday. The" World " tells the story how the fortune was made. The M'Calmonts had been drivenfrom Scot- land as Covenanters, and had settled in Belfast. Mr. M'Calmont's father had two uncles, who were partners in a stockbroking business for .53 years, when the elder, who had married a sister of rue first Lord Cairns, died, leaving his share to his survit,ing brother. This brother never married, and bequeathed the whole of his fortune in trust for his great nephew, now owner of Isinglass, into 'whose Lands it was to pass on his reaching3the age of two and thirty. --- Miss Olive Schreiner, who arrived a few days ago from South Africa, has begun her visit to England by a short residence at Brighton. She will by and by return to London, where society will do its work in lionising her, especially since she has taken to writing on a rather absurd form of mystical religion. .1 remember vending an evening in her company some years ago, on the occasion of herfirst visit to England mborily after the publication of that most charming book a hers, "My African Farm." I was then much impressed with the marvellous]

retiringne,s and seif-a-ffacement of her demeanour. noticed that if it was possible for her to get a back seat during the evening she was to be found there or in some quiet corner, where, surrounded by friends or admirers, she talked with great modesty and agreeableness. and listened more readily than she tilted. There was no trace of consciousness that she had written a book which

had been widely terbiwed and read, and which charmed every one who looked into it. I could at that time, detect no trace of that unwholesome ten.lency to think Or tdik about sacred things which has lutely manifp,ted. Surely it cannot be the lonely life of :he vel,!l, ,No productive of introspec- tion and reverent :q.if-communing, that has led to such opposite reult iu her cabs.

LADIES' LETTE

MY DEAREST NELLIE, You will have seen that blouses ccmtkua to be much worn, and the new models are Weedingly pretty.. Many of these have gracefully-araped shoulder-frills or small capes, but the veryl latest kind is the so-called " handkercnief," or " cross- over," blouse, the fronts of which are cut long enough to cross in front, pass beneath the arms, and tic at the back of the waist in a large flat bow. This blouse can be made of any thin fabric, and takes about 5i yards of single-width material, but it is not easy to make one successfully without a thoroughly good pattern. Plaid silk blouses made on close-fitting linings are worn with black or dark. coloured skirts, and for ultra-smart occasions accordion-pleated chiffon blouses, with silk sleeves, have been brought out. The latest fancy in blouses I have seen is a shaded kind, made of accordion-pleated silk, and ranging in tint from green through white to a! bright rose-pink—a new colour, already known as " 31ey," in compliment to the Royal bride-elect. They are unlined and delightfully cool, if a little showy ir appearance.

The seaside season will aeon be with us, and doubtless you are beginning to agitate your mind as to the most suitable dress for holiday wear. A most serviceable dress can be made in a material of fast-dyed indigo blue sail cloth with vest under- sleeves of white "Irish flax," smocked in indigo blue washing silk. This will look very pretty with a sailor's hat, and will be equally serviceable for yachting. There are at present so many shapes in hats and bonnets that it will ba hard if you cannot find something to please your taste. Very coarse straws appear to be decidedly more popular this season than finer kinds, and the most favoured colour is undoubtedly the golden tan, known as "sunburnt," especially in combination with black lace and pink roses. A pretty example, for instance, has a wide brim drooping slightly over the fore- head, and a low crown encircled by a wreath of close-set damask roses without foliage; a small cluster of the flowers is arranged beneath the brim at the back, where it is just bent up a trifle, and in front there is a large butterfly bow of accordion- pleated black lace and a couple of tall and slender jet ornaments. Another fashionable style of hat is of the same straw, but the brim is divided down the centre of the front, and-the corners thus formed turned sharply back, the gap being filled by loops of black satin ribbon And lace, from which rise two roses (one pale salmon and the other" deep crimson), with very long stalks and plenty of their own leaves. This cleft brim has an exceediuzly stylish etreat and suits most faces.

A fashionable shaped bonnet is covered with heliotrope and gold brocade, and edged with tiny purple cord. The velvet strings and bow are also in a rich shade of purple, with a buckle in paste and jet, and an ostrich tip, shaded from purple to heliotrope. Such a bonnet is charming with a black gown, or the same model may be copied in vieux-rose and olive velvet, or in blotting-paper pink and madderbrown. Simplicity in dresses seems out of date. Every- thing is overloaded with trimming, especially the bodices. The waists are left round and small, but the shoulders and bust are hidden with lace collets, berthes, &c. Even yokes are embroidered or fulled; and guipure both Alto and, cream coloured, heavy make and light make, all are used, but the effect M often good, with the—quaint shades now in favour, such as willow-green,"Delft blue, lavender green, and pale violet. All skirts are made round, just clearing the ground, a stiff' hem lining causing them to fall into the round pleats. Amongst the toilettes that have attracted me most was one of porcelain or Delft blue crepon with a wide flounce of Loie Fuller silk gauze, the bodice ornamented with ficelle guipure embroidered

_ with a little gold ; large gauze sleeves to match the flounce, puffed from the top to the wrist, and confined with blue ribbon bands. A foulard costume, with blue and white pattern, had a white satin sash; a Leghorn straw hat was worn with this dress, the brim fluted and ornamented with large cornflowers and black feathers. A pretty dress was made of ribbed Phebus silk shaded with all the colours of the rain- bow; the large sleeves were in plain silk, pink and blue predominating in the shades, and these seemed gradually to deepen in tone on the bodice. As to the collets, there were not two alike—some long, others short, and a great variety of materials used.

A very pretty evening gown for a youthful matron is in a Louis XIII. style of apricot satin, brocaded with a design of honeysuckle*. The bodice is tabbed, and the skirt opens over a front of white satin, embroidered in harmonising shades. The sash, vest, and trimmings of the puffed sleeves are in cream Liberty satin. The muslin fichu is edged with French tinselled lace, and the cuffs are trimmed to correspond. Yours sincerely, Jaxivut.

Ness.: Put a plate of conlmon salt beneath the bed, and the fleas will soon disappear.

Butter Scotch.

One cup of treacle, two cupfuls of sugar, 1.4 teaspoon- fuls ot soda, one teaspoonsful vanilla, and a piece of butter the size of an egg. Boil without stirring, until it hardens on a spoon. Pour out on buttered plates to cool. Always add the flavouring last.

Paste for Papering. Take a quantity of flour (according to amount. of paste required), mix with a little cold water, and pour over boiling water, stirring all the time. Alum may be added in proportion of lb. to a quartern of , flour. A permanent paste can be made by adding to ' each half- pint of flour paste, without alum, fifteen grains of corrosive sublimate, previously rubbed to powder in a mortar, the whole to be well mixed ; this, it being pre- vented from drying by keeping in a covered pot, re- mains good any length of time ; unfortunately, it is poisonous, but its nauseous taste would no doubt pre- vent it being swallowed accidentally.

The Homely Girl. 'There are a great Many people in this world, more indeed than many of us even ever imagine, who are extremely fond of homely girls. And there is a nios excellent reason for this. Those of us-who go about the world with our eyes open have found it an easy matte to discover that pretty girls depend a great deal o their looks for the esteem in which they are held, indeed many of them presume a great Aeal on this very feet, and with good reason. Somebody has some time ot other said in their hearing that May or 4ennie hadn't the most agreeable manners in the world, latitreally was; so pretty that he couldn't very well fled fault with her; And this had been quite enough. It isn't easy for even: good-dispositioned people always to restrain impatience and ill-temper, even when they knew that the opinions: of those about them demand it, but once they get the; impression that all sorts of things will be forgiven them' becauss they are pretty, the causes are at work which almost inevitably make a pretty girl well-night intoler- able. "The homeliest woman I ever saw said an elderly lady not long since, "was the most Charming in manner, and in time I found her the Mot lovable. When shP was a child she was for many yeara made very unhappy by her personal appearance, as her schoolmates were never weary of telling her of her unprepossessing looks; but she became highly educated and accomplished and mad, herself so amiabl.• and obliging, useful, bright, and every way agreeable, that she was .by all odds the favourite in circles where she is known. If there as anything of interest going on, she was sure to be in the front rank, and if any pleasure was in store she never failed to get her share of it. It became a sort of saying ia her neighbourhood that she was worth a dozen pretty girls any day.' Children should early be taught i that while beauty s always desirable, it is aa unfortun- ate possession when it is allowed to take the place of amiability and sweetness of disposition.

It was strangely prolonged, rising and falling, and having in it the inflection of Mortal agony. Georgina nearly fainted away at once. Gwen came near dropping her lantern. She leaned against the damp wall, half stupefied and bewildered. ' "Perhaps you believe in ghosts now," whispered Georgina, half spitefully. "It must be an owl," suggested Gwen, who knew nothing whatever about owls, save that they frequent dark places and emit a mounful cry. "I am sure it was an owl 1" She called again more clearly than before. And back came that strange muffled cry, that aight•bave come from the depths of a grave.— "Is that an owl?" asked Georgina. They had gone but a little distance when the sepulchral cry was repeated, far more faintly than heretofore. Georgina replied to it by a shriek, and, dropping Gwen's arm, fled like a deer into the black- ness aeead. And Gwen bearing the lantern, ran after her lest she should lose herself emotg the passages. Their good fortune led them, back, after one or two deviations, to the great stone staircase, and they flew up stairs in a very panic. They did not pause in their flight until they reached the kitchen, where Georgina dropped upon a settle, gasping for breath. Gwen set down her lantern and sank upon a chair, lauehing yet trembling. "It was an owl, Georgina," she exclaimed. "We are foolish children!" "We are not foolish!" cried Georgina, resentfully. "You call a ghost an owl, and stick to your belief In face of facts, It Was a ghost—oh, great Heaven, what's that? It's the ghost again 1" A heavy tread was heard in an adjoining room, and the next moment a stout figure appeared in the door- way—the figure of Lord Darkwood. His fat, oily face was aflame with anger. His small, sinister eyes. gleamed like those of a serpent. He was the incarnation of rage, and Gwen shrank away from him in a sudden aversion. His mask had slipped aside, revealing something in that unguarded moment of his real character. "What does this mean 7" he ejaculated, looking from one to the other of the two girls. "Who took the keys of the rains from my cabinet? What are you two doing here?" "Oh 1" cried Georgina, in relief. "It's not a ghost 1" "I desire an explanation I" exclaimed the marquis, angrily. "We came, my lord," said Gwen, with that haughty sweetness of manner that compelled res- pect and deference, "to look through the ruins, as strangers until lately have been allowed to do. Georgina obtained the keys." "Yes, I did," declared Lord Darkwood's daughter. "I found them in your cabinet, father. I should have asked for them only you weren't there, and the thing was of no importance. Only think! we have never been in the ruins until to-day, and we have heard the ghost!" "The ghost!" echoed Lord Darkwood, leaning against the wall.• "Yes," cried Georgina, eagerly. "We- were in the vaults below." Lord Dark wood uttered an exclamation that sounded like a malediction. "Yes," assented Georgina, "and we heard a groan, or scream, or something. I say it was a ghost, but Marian says it was an owl. The idea of it being an owl 1" Lord Darkwood sfas in the shadow, so neither of the girls marked this .sickly yellow pallor of his vis- age. "Miss Myner has more sense than you, Georgina," he said, presently. "It was an owl. The ruins are infested with them, owing to the dense growth of trees close to the walls. And so you were frightened by an owl? What a brave young woman." His sneer irritated his daughter, who declared vehemently that she could distinguish between a ghost and an owl, and that she knew that she had heard a ghost. • " Hush 1 Geogina," said her father. "I won't bear another word of such nonsense. There is no such thing as a ghost. You have heard an owl— nothing more. If you persist in your childish folly, I'll send you to a convent." Georgina saw that her father was in earnest, and she knew that when he was in earnest he was a she very unpleasant person to deal with. Therefore sullenly relinquished her own private opinion and acquiesced in his. • "And now," said Lord Darkwood, "go back to your own quarters. Georgina, I positively for- bid you to enter these ruins again unless I accom- pany you. Miss Myner, I hall depend upon you to keep yourcharie within pro Per bounds." He ,addressed no reproof especially to Gwen— possibly that haughty little head was held too high for his reproofs—but she understood that she was prohibited equally with her charge from again visiting the castle ruins. The marquis arose, and took up the keys, which lay on the floor at Gwen's feet. He extinguished the light of the lantern; and said politely— " Young ladies, permit me to show you the way out." He led the way, the girls following, to a rusty, creaking little door that led from a passage off the servant's hall to a ruined little walled-in garden beyond, and let them out., He secured the door behind them, crossed the garden, and opened a door in the wall. They all passed out, and he locked this door also. "Now," he,said, "you can find your way through the park homeward. I trust this will be the last of this ghost-seeing folly. We will leave the old ruin to the owls." The girls hurried away together, and Lord Dark- wood halted iii the shadow on the wall, and looked after them with a strange and ugly smile.

Pietro and his master alike began to believe that the girl was either dead or had quitted England. Tile marquis was thinking of Miss Winter on this night, in the solitude of the library, anti specu- lating as to her whereabouts. Thus he deliberated with a proper sense of his own merits. _ _ _ _

MAN-RATING TIGERS.

A correspondent of the Allahabirl "Pioneer," writing on man-eating tigers, mentions the career of a young tigress Who in the space of nine months killed some dozens of human beings, depopulated several villages, and stopped work over a greater part of a forest division, in spite of the most strenu- ous efforts made to destroy her—eff 3rts increased by the incentive of R 5.500 reward offered for her head. She began her career in July by killing two women near a forest village, and by the end of the following December had killed at 'vast 30 persons, becoming bolder and more cunning with each fresh mur- der. Her beat lay in the foot hills of the Himalayas, and she roamed over an area 25 miles long by three or four broad. The country was such that rise could neither be tracked for any distance nor driven by elephants or beaters. She would not kill a tied buffalo, nor would she go back to a corpse if once disturbed. She became at last so bold that she would in open day- light carry off men and women when cutting the crops in the terraced fields, stalking the unfortunates from above and suddenly springing on them, The terror of her ferocity spread through the country. The villagers left their homes for safer regions ; and no wonder, for the writer found on one occasion a village seemingly deserted,- but in reality in a state of siege, the inhabit- ants being afraid to draw water from a stream a hun- dred yards from their houses. Work was commencing in'the forests, and in a short time the tigress learned to stalk the sound of an axe, and made many victims before the forest was proved to be even more dangerous than the fields had been. The method of attack adopted was so sudden as to prevent any possibility of escape, the blow dealt an deadly as to render even a cry for help impossible. The victim was dead and carried off before his companions knew what had occurred. The most strenaous efforts were made hailer destruction—poison, spring guns, and dead falls were ineffectually resorted to, any number of buffaloes were tied up at night, and many a time the flesh trail of a kill was taken up in hopes of obtaining a shot at the tigress, but with no further result than that of recovering a mangled or half- eaten corpse. During January and February this tigress had killed 15 to 20 more persons, and at last a file of , soldiers were requisitioned to see what force could do to , remove this horrible animal,cunning having been found of no avail. On being killed she was found to be a young animal, in perfect condition ; the pad of her left forefoot had at one time been deeply cut from side to side, but had thoroughly healed, leaving, however, a deep scar, which proved her presence wherever she roamed. The writer mentions one instance in which two cowherds, living in a small grass hut in a somewhat wild forest, were cooking their food in the evening, when a tiger suddenly sprang on one and carried him off. His corn- 1 panion intimidated the animal with shouts and threats, and succeeded in making him leave his victim. Carry- ing his wounded companion into the hut, the man closed the entrance and waited for daylight. But this he never saw, for after a time the tiger, emboldened by the increasing darkness, returned, and forcing his way into the but, carried off the uninjured man, who was doubt- less doing all he could to prevent the tiger's approach. The other who was first seized died of his wounds and of terror the next day, after relating the story to those who had found him. "Of the man-eaters I have known, none have been old or decrepit animals, driven to feed on human beings becuse they could not obtain other food. They lived in a country fish of game- and where 'cattle were plentiful ; but they had lost their fear of man, and, trusting"' io-their aupeCiOr .strengtti and cum-- ning, had no difficulty in satisfying ,Cheii, hudger. At the same time, they seemed -to redegnise that an armed man was dangerous, and that they. must be doubly on their guard to avoid falling into a trap. It is this appar- ent knowledge of man and his habits, amounting in some instances almost to reason, that renders a man- eating tiger so terrible."

SOME STORIES OF HISSING.

In December, 1810, the police of Copenhagen issued the following curious ordinance : "After this present notice, the public shall not testify their dissatisfaction at: the conclusion of a plege at the theatre until ten minutes after the fall of the cur- tain. At the expiration of these ten minutes, a signal will be given by three beats on a great drum, and all those who after that shall hiss, or give any other mark of disapprobation, will be arrested as dis- turbers of the public peace. 'A French newspaper of the same year (from which this ordarnance is translated) says that it was infringed the very first night it was in force, and that arrests were made accoldingly. The fact that hissing is reckoned legitimate at the theatres, has led men to choose them as the places for expressing their public dislikes in times of great excitement. Shakespeare's Cardinal Wolsey was hissed at the time of the Papal aggression, but the hiss was not meant for the actor, but for Cardinal Wiseman. Hisses are directed at unpopular persons who come as spectators and not as actors. Sir William Knighton says that George IV. always entered the theatre with an excessive dread ot being saluted with this mark of public disap- probation. If he heard one single hiss, although it were immediately drowned in general and tumultuous ap- plause, he went home wretched, and would lie awake all night thinking of that one ugly note, not of the thou- sand agreeable notes. Sometimes it has not been one visitor, but a whole party of visitors who would have had the hisses of the spectators directed upon them. In one of the periodical "essays," poor imitations of the 1" Tatler" and "Spectator,' which appeared in such numbers throughout the eighteenth century (the "Prater," 1756, re-published as a hook in 1757), we are told that the conduct of ladies in the theatres was often so unbecoming, that the audience hissed them into silence. It seems that they talked and laughed 80 loudly as to render the actors inaudible.

A chapter might bea made Upot;--the repartees of the victims el hissing. To say that the hissed have often given back as good as they got, would he to say. that they merely showed light; but the fact is that they have very frequently, like Orator Hunt, won an unmis- takable victery. On one occasion there were only seven persons in the theatre at Weimar; the seven, however, considered themselves to form a sufficient court of criticism, and taking offence at the had acting of one performer, they hissed him energetically; the manager thereupon brought his whole company upon the stage and nut-hissed the visitors. Mr. H. C. Robinson tells us that he was present at Covent Garden Theatre with Charles and Mary Lamb in Deceifiber 1806, when Lamb's H ll—was performed for the first time. The absurdity of the piece turns upon the hero being ashamed of his name, which is only revealed at the end as" Hogs-flesh." The prologue was very well received," says ,Mr. Robinson, "indeed, it could not fail, being one. of the very best in our language. But on the disclosure of the name, the squeamishness of the vulgar taste in the pit showed itself by hisses; and I recollect that Lamb joined, and was probably. the loudest hisser in the house." Rossini. at the first performance of his famous le Barbiere di Seviglia, took the very opposite course; when every one was hissing, he turned round and ener- getically applauded. H. felt certain of the triumphant future of the opera, and from his earliest youth was un- moved by the first judgment of the general public.

It Was froin Mrs. Quillet, and bad arrived under enclosure from Mrs. Myner. The Lonemoor housekeeper was not an apt letter writer. This was the Second or third ephitle Gwen had received from her since the, girl's arrival ~at Dunkolm Castle; lint this lettire communicated Important news... ' Squire Markham had returned home after his seventeen years' wandering. "And he's that changed," wrote Mrs. Quillet, in her uncertain, straggling hand, " that I should scarcely have known him if I'd met him in a strange :place. He's grey and sorrowful, poor squire; and :I well remember the day when he was the srnilingest, ipleasantest gentleman in all Yorkshire. He's old ;now, and shows more than his years. Trouble has ,been bard upon him. "He drove over from Penistone, and I could hardly believe my own eyes when he entered his own gates. He came into the house slow and 'anxious.' He met Meg at the door, and started back at sight of her, as if he was afraid. He came into my room and called to me. I knew his voice, end oh' Miss Gwen, you can 'imagine how I wel- corned back the dear old master; in spite of his hardness and harshness of these later years!" Mrs. Quillet did not mention that Squire Mark- ham had not asked after his daughter's child—that he had avoided questioning the housekeeper until after dinner, and then, catching a glimpse of stout, brawny Meg, as she stumbled about the kitchen offices, he had asked, huskily— ' "Is that the girl ? " _

"No, sir," the housekeeper had answered; "that ix Meg, the dairymaid." "The girl is dead ? " "No, sir, she is living," said Mrs. Quillet, nervously, hoping to win Squire Markham's pro- tection for her nursling. She is a young lady now, squire, and very handsome, too, they call her."

"A young lady ?" echoed Squire Markham, with a sneer.

"Yes, sir. You see, squire," faltered the old housekeeper, tremblingly, "being who she was, I couldn't send her to a foundling, nor I couldn't snake a servant of her. I had a governess to educate her, and we sent her to a Paris pensionnat, and she learned music, and the languages, and the use of the globes." The squire muttered an oath. "You have played the fool," he exclaimed. "Where is this fine lady of your rearing ? " "She is gone out to be a governess, sir, in a great family." . .

"So much the better. Never speak to me of her again. Never allow her to come here again. I won't have her in this house. You have wasted money upon her. Look to her for your pay, for I swear I will never pay one penny for an outcast like that. I washed my hands of her at the out- set. What you have done for her is your doing, not mine." .

And with this the squire had stalked away, leaving poor Mrs. Quillet in utter despair. We will allow her to tell what happened next in her own language in her letter to Gwen.

"I resolved, Miss Gwen, to appeal to the squire in your behalf, for he has neither chick nor child, and might choose to give you a home under this roof, in this house in which you chanced to be born. But it cannot be.

"After dinner, just as I was thinking to approach the squire on my errand, the bailiff comes in and goes to the library where the squire is sitting. And Mc. Orkney presently tells the master that I brought up the child of that beggar woman to be mistress of Lonemoor, that I gave her the name of Gwendoline Winter, that I lavished money on her, and that the girl had turned out had.

"The squire called me in, and Mr. Orkney tells this again to my face. I deny it—that is, that you are bad—but Orkney says he'll prove his words, and that you are a liar, a schemer, a false, wicked, treacherous creature. And the squire, paying no attention to what I say, mutters: 'Bad blood 1 bad blood !"

"All I can say goes for nothing. The squire believes Orkney and suspects my motives. And he orders me never to admit you again to Lonemoor, on pain of my instant dismissal. That Orkney has everything his own way. Master believes him; I am thought a weak, false creature. "I have told you all this, Miss Gwen, plainly, because I must tell you I had hoped to interest the squire in you; but the'Orkneys have made him hate you. You can never come back to Lonemoor. You had better settle down in your governess-life for good and all. If John and I were to leave our good home, we would not be fit protectors for you. You must take care of yourself, and may heaven take care of you." Gwen leaned forward an laid the letter on the fire, watching it burn to ashes. "What could have led her to appeal to Squire Markham in my behalf ? " she asked herself. "I would not return to Lonemoor under any circum- stances: I would not accept his protection. As to the Quillets, I will repay them to the uttermost farthing all the money they have expended upon me. I have laid by every penny of my salary BO far for them, and I shall continue to do so. The child of that beggar-girl who died upon the moor may be an outcast, but she is independent!"

She arose and laid aside her pretty woollen dress, and put on a cashmere dressing-gown. She was wakeful, and, after sitting awhile longer by her fire, extinguished her light and sat down at her window, _

It will be remembered that this window looked out upon, a' lovely garden, and across it upon the ruins of the ancient entitle. Gwen mused awhile upon her <win affairs, upbn the letter she had just destroyed, and then her thoughts settled upon her present life and the singular cry she had heard in the castle dungeons. 'I wish I had looked over the 'Cyclopedia Britannica,' " she thought, " and studied upon owls. I wonder if owls do live in dungeons? There may be a way for them to get down through seine crevice in the wall. And why should an owl make such a human sort of cry? Of course, it was an owl I believed so at the time, and I never thought to doubt it until Lord Darkwood asserted that it was an owl. I must be innately perverse, for when he asserted it I began to doubt it. Yet what else could it be? It wasn't a ghost. I don't believe in ghosts, and I shouldn't believe in them if I saw one.' • • She looked at the ruins in idle curiosity. The night was neither dark nor light, but the Shadows lay thickly on the old grey walls opposite. "Midnight !" said Gwen. "I ought to be in bed-r-ah ! what's that?"

A light had flashed across one of the fower win- dows of the ruins. -

No servant 'could be in that " haunted " pile at that hour. No burglar would have bought for booty there. _ What 'could it mean ?

The light flashed again across a window—another window this time—and again disappeared. • Gwen watched long and anxiously, but it was not seen again. But what could it mean? (To be continued.)

THE FEMALE SAVAGE IN POLITICS.

P. W. writes to the" Daily News" : "I was at the New Gallery on Friday, and 'standing opposite Mr. Sydney Hall's picture of • Mr. Gladstone reading lessons in Hawarden Church." Near me were a middle-aged gentleman and a Medusa-faced lady. • I'm thankful,' ~he said savagely, glaring at Mr. Oladatone's portrait, that he looks very old. Perhaps he won't live many years longer. His hypocritical face is much worn.' I took down her words in my catalogue. Since I heard a lady say many years age that she wished she could see "Mr. Bright dead in her back garden,' I have not met so good a specimen of the political virago. My Gorgon at the New Gallery on Friday launched her shaft so that we all could hear. I am net a Home Ruler, but if I had been one could scarcely have been more disgusted or ashamed ; for, after all. as Mr. A nstey says, • We are both human beings.' I enclose my card, but not for publication, for I have not forgotten the fate of Orpheus at the hands of the Thracian women."

WHAT MR. INGHAM TOLD MR. HEYDEN. It was a very lucky thing for Mr. Heyden that Mr. Ingham called to see him just when be did. But it would have been better still if he had done so long be- fore. For Mr. Ingham turned out to be the only man able to give any advice worth a rush. Lots of other people had talked and suggested things; as they do when they see a house on fire. But it is commonly the fire- men who put out the blaze after all. And so Mr. Ing- ham happened to have a bit of useful knowledge that nobody else had. And indeed the case was very like a fire, although it wasn't a house, you know, it was a man ; namely, Mr. Heyden himself.

Only the day after Christmas (1891) he told the story in these very words. "Fifteen years ago," be said, "in December, 1878, I met with a slight accident and had great pain in my ankle, which at first I thought was sprained. In a few days the pain moved up to my knee, while all the surrounding parts became swollen and puffed up. I could not bear to put my foot on the ground or even let the bed sheet touch the leg. A doc- tor who attended me for two months said it was rheu- matism, and treated me accArdingly. When I got a little better he sent me to Southport for three weeks. I returned to my work again but bad great difficulty in getting about, and from time to time I had to leave work, owing to the intense' pain. Later I had eacruti ating pains in all my limbs, and the joints of my fingers became enlarged and grew out of shap4.

" Then I consulted another doctor who attended me through several severe attacks. He said my complaint was Chalk Gout. He gave me medicines but said he could not do much for me, and that in time the disease would kill me. In this way A continued to suffer for 14 years. During that period I took every gout and rheu- matism medicine I heard of, but nothing gave me more than temporary relief. "In March, 1890, I had a bad attack and was bedfast for over two months, when one night a friend of mine, Mr. James Ingham, of Old Trafford, called to see me. The pain was at its height, and seeing my condition, he said he knew of 'something that would de me good. He brought me a few doses in a bottle but refused to say what it was. It gave me so much relief that I sent my wife to ask him. He replied, I will come and tell him all about it.' He soon came and said it was called Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup. Upon this I told him I had often heard of it, but regarded it as a quack medi- cine. I sent at once to Burgon's stores in Oxford-stand got a bottle, and after using it twenty-four hours felt much better, in a few days I was out of bed and at work, and have never lost a day's work since, nor had any attack of my old enemy. I will gladly answer all inquiries." (Signed) HENRY R. HEYDEN, 28, Booth-st., East, Oxford-road, Manchester.

Now this statement of Mr. Ileyden's is surprising. The reader wants to know how it can be true, and he has a right to ask. The explanation is this :—Mr. Hey- den was afflicted with rheumatic gout, an almost univer- sal complaint, very painful and dangerous. The cause is a poison in the blood produced as follows —First the stomach becomes inactive and torpid with indigestion and dyspepsia ; more work is thus thrown on the liver than it is able to do ; the overloaded liver fails in the manufacture of urea, leaving it in the blood in the form of a solid called uric acid. This acid, a deadly poison, unites chemically with the soda (an alkali) in the blood, forming urate of sodium, a hard crystal poison. This poison goes round in the blood current until it is finally deposited in the muscles and joints,setting them onfre with inflammation and inflicting fearful agony. Continued, the disease causes chalk stones in the bladder, Bright's disease of the kidneys, and disease of the heart and lungs. All comes from the same source, indigestion and dvspepsia, and ate pro- perly symptoms of that ailment. What a pity people don't understand this fact letter. ‘. • - Mother SAgel's Syrup cures by its picullaertul action on the stomach and liver, and thus it cured the case above described. It begins at the right end. Perhaps it would be wise in you to paste this account in your scrap hook, or where you can find it in time of need.

TRE '• TRICKS " OF TIM CYCLE TRADE.

"Caveat ernptor " would seem to be a useful motto to bear in mind when buying cycles no less than in buying ' horses. A correvondent of " Cassell's Saturday Journal" has been worming some of the secrets of the cycle trade from the manager of one of the largest manufactories in the North of England. A large number of machines are made expressly to be sold as second- hand. The great thing is to make it appear they are genuine bargains, and accordingly agents are appointed in all parts to sell them on commission frotn,private houses. "Ladies in want of money must sacrifice per- fectly new machine for one-fifth of original cost " ; " young gentleman going abroad will dispose of beauti- ful machines. with all the latest improvements ; cost £2O ; take £5." Others are "giving up riding," and so forth. A machine for this market can be made for about £2 103. The agents pay £4 10s. for thent, and get what they can from £5 to £lO. The profits there- fore are sufficiently large in both cases. Sometimes old, worn-out machines are painted up, and made to look almost new to the eye. "I remember one such machine," said this too candid cycle maker, " that looked as good as new, but I beard the governor say he was deuced glad to see it round the first corner."

THE INCREASE OF LUNACY IN

LANCASHIRE.

The quarterly meeting of the Lancashire Asylums Board was held on Wednesday at Preston. The Lunacy Commissioners Wrote strongly urging the necessity for additional accommodation being provided for lunatics in the county, and the Sites Committee reported recom- mending three sites from which a selection could be made for building a new asylum. Power was given to the Committee to negotiate for the purchase of a site. Authority was given to the Rainhilt Asylum Committee to arrange with the proprietor of the Haydock Lodge private asylum to take a hundred and fifty patients pro- viding the necsssity should become pressing, and the Board granted a sum not exceeding £32,030 to provide reception and other wards for 200 additional patients at Whittingham Asylum.

A DETECTIIT'S STRUGGLE Wl2ll A

DESERTER

The Liverpool police have recently been in quest of a man named Michael Traynor, 21 years of age, who was wanted for desertion and stealing £6 belonging to his regiment, stationed at Woolwich. On Sunday last Detectives Robbins and Cowan heard of the prisoner being at a house in a court off Roscoe-street, and went there to arrest him, When the accused heard of the approach of thepolice he pulled a window frame from it place and flung it at the officers,' injurin„o' Cowan. Traynor then dropped from the opening and made his escape. On Wednesday he was traced to Saltport by Detective Robbins and Detective Leybourne, and when he saw the officers he made a hasty retreat. Being closely pressed by the officers he jumped into the river Weaver. Leybourne also plunged in, and after a desper- ate struggle brought Traynor to the shore, where be was secured and conveyed to the Liverpool brideweU

FRAUD ON WARRINGTON MINISTERS.

THE IMPOSTER SMARTLY CAUGHT.

On Monday there arrived in Warrington a respectably dressed individual who made is, his business to see several of the Nonconformist iifllB, ters in the town, in order to tell them of his necuniary difficulties, thereby hoping to touch their hearts and unloose their purse strings with his , pitiful tale. , His mode of action, .to say the.ltaast, . Wad 'claver; but in this case not- clevet enough to succeed with one rev, gentleman whom he tried to dupe. He commenced " operations " on MondaY morning, by calling upon the Rev. John Yonge, iR Wilson-Patten-street, and said he came as a Con- gregationalist to know if he would recommend hini, to somebody, to give him work, and described himself as an engineer's storekeeper. After delivering his elabor- ately prepared tale in which he mentioned several well- known Congregational ministers in Newcastle-on-Tyne, Birmingham, and other towns with whom he said he was intimately acquainted, Mr. Yonge recommended ' him to the manager of a firm of engineers in the town,- but not being successful, be again called at Mr. Yonge's house, but fortunately for that gentleman he was out. He afterwards wended his way to the Rev. J. W. Varley house, and after passing himself off as a staunch Baptist, he related the the same story as he had previously related to Mr.Yonge, but was careful to sub- stitute the names of various Baptist ministers in the towns named. The man's appearance and statements reminded the rev, gentleman ot a paragraph he had " read in a well-known Nonconformist journal, written by a frieed of his, residing at Derby, warning ministers against being defrauded, and Mr. Varies accordinal9 told the man to call again at 2.30. Mr. Varley wrote to his friend in Derby and sent as good a description of the man as he could to him, and ha received a telegram that it was the same man. In the meantime Mr. Varley met Mr. Yonge and learned from him that he had had a morning visitor answering the same description. Promptly at the appointed time the man again put in an appearance, and Mr. Varley gave him a letter of introduction to the engineer on the Acton Grance section of the Manchester Ship Canal, la ho might possibly find him some employmept, and told him to again call the following day it he did not succeed. Mr. Varley after- , wards made a statement to the Chief Constable and also to Detective Monaghan, and made arrangements for the .4 latter to be at his house on Tuesday morning. Instead , of going to Acton Grange with the letter, the man thought it would be far more profitable to visit the Rev. J.4Warnock, Presbyterian minister, this time represent- ing himself to be a Presbyterian, and citing the names of various ministers of that denomination. His tactics , succeeded, for Mr. Warnock gave him a shilling W relieve his apparent distress. Elated with this niece of good luck he determined to prosecute his endeavours still further, and this time went to Rose Cottage, Latch- ford, to see Councillor Henry Roberts; J.P.,and told him , that he had spent all his money in Birmingham seeking for work,and that he had travelle lon to Wolverhampton, Stafford, Crewe, and Warrington. Ile also told him be had a letter of introduction to an engineer in the Shin ' Canal, and led Mr. Roberts to think that it was from someone outside Warrington. When about to go he said he didn't know what he vets going to do, as he had 110 money. Mr. Roberts afterwards handed him a shilling. The following day he again called upon Mr. Valley, but Detective Monaghan who had been waiting some time for his arrival had left, and therefore Mr. Varley made arrangements For him to go to the Horemarket-street Coffee Tavern, as the man said he had had no dinner, and also arranged for Detective Monaghan to meet him there, At the Coffee Tavern, the stranger was regaled with refreshments, and during the meal Monaghan joined the company and entered into conversation with the interesting visitor on various topics, and ultimatelY suggested a walk through the town. Of course D.S. Monaghan desired to see the" Mansion" in Irlam-st., and accordingly they leisurely strdlled up Scotlano-rd., Buttermarket-st., and Irlam-st. On arrival at the police station, the consternation of the stranger may be better imagined than described when he found he was being "run in." When searched a hook was found upon hist containing a large number of names of ministers in various districts including Warwickshire, Northsvlch, Crewe, and Warrington. On Wednesday, at the Borough Court, before Mr. John Fairelough (in the chair) and Mr. John Geddes, the prisoner was charged with obtaining money bY false pretenees. Prisoner after some hesitation pleaded 'a guilty. The Chief Constable in his opening statement said the prisoner had evidently been " working the circuit." After evidence by the Rev. John Yonge who said ministers were victimized very considerably by this sort of thing, and said he thought that when a case was proved up to the hilt it was only right that it should be brought under the notice of the Bench. The Revs. J.,. W. Varley. and J. Warnock, Councillor H. Roberts, aput : Detective Monaghan also gave evidence. The prisoner when answering in his defence said ha had nothing tr say, hut asked to be treated leniently. He had made every honest effort to obtain work, and when be went'. to Mr. Varley and also to the others, he went with the purest intentions of simply asking them to recommen_d him work. He also though he didn't give Mr. Robert"' . In understand that the letter was from outside Warriug- ton. Prisoner who had already served two months f_.,01. a Similar offence was sent to gaol for three months' ha r; labour: The Chairman thought the prisoner's mode getting a livelihood was very dishononrable. The beam' were also much obliged to Mr. Varley and the other gentlemen for attending.

THE DOWAGER DUCHESS RELEASED.

The Dowager Duchess of Sutherland was released from Holloway Gaol on Tuesday. She was tollowed to her house near Windsor by a "deputation," who presented " a small token " of their sympathy, in the shape of a silver casket containing a cheque for the amount of the fine in-loosed on the Duchess by Sir F. Jeune, and bear- ing an inscriptisn denunciatory of that learned judge. Most unfortunately the Duchess was not able to receive the "deputation," but she " d,si red" the PITS 9 Associa- tion to express her acknowledgments to the people who have been touched by her sorrows and sufferings. The Press Association special correspondent inquired as to the health of her Grace, and was reassured to learn that she is on the whole cheerful but " has lost a great amount of weight." The Duchess has been interviewed by a lady reporter of the " Westminster Gazette," to whom she made some remarkable statements with re- gard to the destroyed document. It referred—so she says—to a "grave scandal in the family" which occurred years ago.

HER ACCOUNT OF TUE DESTROYED LETTER..

----- A few minutes after twelve o'clock on Monday the Duchess of Sutherland, accompanied by Mr. and Mrs. Worthington, drove into the gates of her idyllic river- side retreat, The Willows, having been driven all the way from Holloway. Her welcome was very quiet. Two young girls came out, were greeted with a gentle smile and an affectionate embrace, and went into the house with the rest of the party. A friendly parrot showed signs of delight at the return of its mistress, and a couple of footmen bowed to her grace as she went through the picturesque hall. Five minutes later (writes a " Lady Representative" of the " Westminster Gazette '') the duchess received me in a shady nook of the garden. She was still dre..sed in deep mourning, and had only exchanged her black bonnet for a small white sailor hat. Her only ornament was a magnificent string of pearls, looking more gleaming and beautiful for the contrast they formed tot e stiiihied simplicity of the rest of the costume. The duchess is a woman of queenly figure, but as she walked slowly across the sunny lawn it was very evident that the six weeks' confiner.. nit in Holloway Gaol has indeed " told on her." She looks thin, her white hands litvu a nervous twitch, and there is a look of pain and weariness about her ' face. Quiet and very gently she said, after a few words of greeting,' lam very glad you have come, for I think it is justa little hard that all the papers, without ex- ception, have published column after column criticising and condemning my action without ever making an at- tempt to hear or state the other side. I will tell you simply the facts about the whole affair. I will tell it you as I would tell it before God, truthfully, and with- out additions of any kind. " When the duke lay on his death bed in September last, he said to me one afternoon, in the presence of icy brother and Mr. Worthlngton and other., I want you to look up, from among my papers, a certain bundle of letters, and to destroy them all. You must search for them in London and at our country places till you find them. 'Then burn them.' And the same evening, quite late, when I was all alone with my husband, he talked about the letters again, and said My darling, you only shall do this for me, amid I know you will do it.' Of course I promised, and I meant to keep my pro- mise. I should explain th it these letters referred to a very very grave family scandal which happened many years before I catne into the family, and which the late duke was anxious to suppress, se that no more trouble should arise from it for any " All that has been said about the haste and hurry, and attempts at concealing the note which I destroyed, is absolutely and entirely untrue. Here are the facts. Last March—and please take note that it was only in March that I Was allowed to look through the late duke's papers, though he died in September—we were search- ing for the bundle of letters which I had promised my dying husband to des`roy. The letters could not be found, but in my search for them I came upon another letter which lay somewhat crumpled under a number of other papers. It looked, I not iced,as if it had stuck at the back of a drawer, as he tem sometimes do. I opened it ; it was from the Duke of Sutherland to me before we were married. There had been a great many letters,for we were engaged tor nine months. I looked at it. There was nothing on the first page of special interest, but when I turned to the next page I found something re- ferring to this grave family scandal. Not, mind you, the actual letters I was told to destroy, but a letter re- , ferring to this scandal, which, I knew, my husband would wish me to destroy. So after reading it I quietly folded it, put it beside me on the table, and presently walked slowly across the room and put it into the fire. It is not true that I rushed across. I had a trailing dress on, for one thing, which would have prevented me from "rushing." And then, I am not in the habit of doing things in a hurry. When those in the room saw what I was doing, one man raised some slight protest, and then for an hour and a half we went on working. It was only three days after that all this ridiculous fuss was made. What downright nonsense it was, to say that I burned papers relating to icy dear husband's will! Why, that will is a heavy, bulky document, and the letter I burnt was a sheet of notepaper. " I see now," the duchess went on presently, with a sad look and a very low voice, "I see now that it would have been wi,er if I had not destroyed the letter. I should have saved myself all this torture and indignity then. If I had only thought, ' What does it matter to me ? Let the family scandals be known.' I personally had nothing whatever to do with the matter ; it could not harm me if all were known, far, as I said before, it happened eight or nine years before I came into the family, only I had promised my dear husband in his last hours to keep this scandal from coming up again, and I would not have the woman I am if I had not been faithful twhis dying wish. And for this reason lam glad I destroyed the letter, though if I were more worldly wise I would not have done it." " Is it true that your health has suffered by your imprisonment ?"—" Yes, that is quite true. My doctor says that never in his practice has he seen such a change in anybody but a consumptive person. lam very tall, you see, and when I went to Holloway Gaol I weighed over 12st. I have lost over 2st. during those six weeks. The loneliness wasi terrible." " But I understood that you were allowed to see your friends whenever you liked ? "—" Indeed not. I was allowed three visitors a week. My mother came once, my brother once, and Mr. Worthington once. Not a single one of the prison rules was relaxed any more than it would have been for any lady, however humble her position might be. But one thing I must say, and that is that every one of the prison officials, from the highest to the lowest, was most kind and considerate, and res- pectful. I have absolutely nothing to complain of in this respect. Only prison is prison, and the one thing which helps me to bear it is the thought that I have done what the dear duke would have had me to do. I could say much more, but it will be better to say very little just now. I have hopes that time will clear it all up, and that I shall yet be justified, even before the world." Then we walked to where my trap was waiting, the duchess still nervously playing with the rings on her hands, and saying once more, " I have told you the simple truth."

‘ -4p--- THE EMPLOYERS' LIABILITY BILL.

The Standing Committee on Law resumed considera- tion of the Employers' Liability Bill on Thursday. Dis- cussion was continued on clause 2, and Mr. W. M'Laren proposed an amendment which raised the question whether workmen should be allowed to contract them- selves out of the measure if they could get something better. Great insurance societies like those established by the London and North-Western Railway Company were on the whole advantageous to the men, but if the clause remained unaltered it would kill such societies. Mr. Asquith said the Government had no hostility to such insurance funds when they provided adequate compensation for injured workmen, but they felt that the only safe plan was to continue on the lines laid down in the bill. The amendment in effect said that the men would determine by ballot under the supervision of a Government official whether they would prefer insurance or the bill. The amendment if carried would he applicable to every workshop, mine, or factory in the country, and in many Nf the smaller undertakings the protection of th,e ballot would be futile. It would be known how the men had voted, and the influences brought to bear on them to contract away their statutory rights would be overwhelming. He pointed out tht clause 3 had been specially designed to prevent injury to such funds, and said he meant to stand by it with the amendment that if a workman got a verdict the amount due to him from the insurance fund established by his employer must be taken into consideration. After along discussion the amendment was negatived by 29 votes to 7, and clause 2 was agreed to.

• Mr. John Redmond, in an interview with a 'press re- presentative, says :—" Gilbert has been released in an incurable state. Mr. Asquith humanely deferred his discharge until arrangements could be made for bis proper treatment, and the man has been removed to a private hospital, where he is provided with the necessary nurses. It is a remarkable fact that the Irish Americans, to whom the position of these men is largely due, have not contributed anything to their relief or to help their friends to Fee them, and in fact the amnesty movement has not received a single farthing from America. Gil- bert's case is one wldch demands aid from the other side. It is a severe commentary upon the imprisonment of these men that of six confined, within a few years two were released on account of their condition and subse- quently died; a third, Gilbert, is dying; and three are insane. These are Dr. Gallagher, Whitehead, and Deveny. lam aware that their insanity is a matter of dispute, but I have satisfied myself that it is an un- questionable fact They are all young men."

1H E EXAMINER

AT OUR LOCAL CaURCUES, WAR itINGTON.

THE P.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE. About 800 men attended the Pleasant Sunday After- noon meeting in Wycliffe Church on Sunday. Thirteen new members were enrolled. Mr. Ziba Armitage, J. P., was the chairman and delivered a brief address on brotherhood. Mr. W. Collier was the speaker, and based his remarks on the words " Slippery Places," Psalm 73. 18 verse. He dwelt upon what he termed four slippery places, viz., bad . companionship, intemperance, gamb- IMg, and impure literature, and showed the danger of each in a concise manner, urging the men to beware of them. [he address which was an excellent one, was listened to with close attention, and elicited applause. The soloist for the afternoon was Mr. W. H. Wallington, who sang two solos in an admirable manner. The Rev. John Yonge and Mr. A. Lang also took part in the ser- vice. COWPER.

The anniversary services— at the Bold-st., Wesleyan Chapel were held on Sunday when the preacher in the morning and evening was the Rev. J. Rowland Gleave, of Fgrernont, and a native of Warrington. The front of the pulpit was tastefully decorated with plants, and a platform was also placed round the pulpit for the children, the girls being in white. Special hymns and anthems were well executed by the choir and children. Mr. Peter Harrison presiding at the organ, and Mr. Pigot having charge of the scholars. During the morn- ing the anthem "0 give thanks " was ably rendered by the choir, Miss A. Rawlins taking the solo portions. In the afternoon, Miss Bessie Dutton gave a very interest- ing address from the words '• Follow me," and in addition to suitable hymns Mr. Levi Richardson sang " When I survey the wondrous cross" in a pleasing manner. In the evening two anthems" Praise the Lord 0 Jerusalem" and "The radiant morn bath passed away" were given in a manner which delighted the large con- gregation. Miss E.Rawlins and Mr. Levi Richardson also contributed solos. The services were well attended and the collections for the day amounted to £52 7e. 114 d. On Monday evening tha Rev. J. R. Gleave gave a lecture entitled " A night with Cowper," the Rev. J Kirtlan taking the chair. The Chairman expressed his pleasure at seeing a good number present, as lectures as a rule, did not take so well in Warrington. He was also pleased that the lecturer was upon a poet some of whose beautiful hymns were enshrined in the Wesleyan hymn- book. Tile Lecturer said that the peculiarities of Cowper's poetry were so strongly marked that his lines could not be mistaken for those of any other poet. Though not to be ranked as a genius of the loftiest order, he must always be allowed an honourable place among British poets. Though he does net startle like Byron and and Shelley nor strike with the exquitite finish of Words werth, he speaks to the human nature, with a greater force than either. Though not qualified for epic or drama as were Milton or Shakespeare he had a wonder- ful way of putting before inen in verse, the result of his own obeervatien. His poetry was autobiographical. His , love and hate, melancholy and mirth, betrayed them- selves in his verses, and as we read them, we could sea him as he was. His poetry was also true to nature. Simplicity constituted the strength and beauty of Cowper's lines. His images were true reflections of the varied works of God, and were never marred by extravagance of thought or expression Great versatility also marked Cowper's poetry. There were contrasts in it as great as ever appeared in any author, teeth as to subj.. ct and style of treatment, and all marked by that intellectual strength, wisdom and purity which peculiarly belonged to him. Sometimes his passages were as though they had been written as specimens of bitter invective, crushing and merciless de- nunciation and scathing satire. There was a keenness and a polish about them which a Disraeli might have envied in his best days. Search where you would, you would never find an uninteresting page, and scarcely ever an unmeaning or commonplace line. Cowper was an ardent lover of mankind, and his poetry breathed the spirit of philanthropy. Be like Shakespeare, believed that man was the nobleet of all God's works, and was nearest the Divine in attributes and nature. When slavery existed in the colonies, and long before there was any public agitation on the question,his soul was fired with indignation at the accused traffic, and he penned words which sounded like a trumpet and called to emanci- pate the captives. Yet with all this, there was a patriotism in him which burned and &wed with an in- tensity never surpassed. Amid the many excellencies of his poems there was the sincerity and purity of his teachings. No one could study him without revering the man, without a deep feeling of the loftiness of his character. The opinions which he expressed were his own, and not the offspring of hearsay, and he spoke forth his noble thoughts because his heart was too full to be silent. The staple of his poetry was truth. The purity of his teachings could not be questioned, and could not be too highly appreciated. In an age when vice was flattered and virtue trampled underfoot in high places, he spike opt fearlessly against the wrong and with a noble eloquence championed the right and the good, and with the most diligent search not a line could ! be found to call forth a blush on the cheek of modesty. tie had been charged with a want of sufficient poetical oneness in his writings, but this very defect arose more from his facility of thought than lack of ability to elaborate the thought. Cowper made religious poetry a power in the land, and swept away the idea that poetry on such themes could never have a permanent place in the literature of England. Little need be said of his hymns as they were so well-known. Among his com- positions were " God moves in a mysterious way," "Oh a closer walk with God," " There is a fountain filled with blood," "I will praise thee every day," " Some- times a light surprises." Mr. Gleave quoted numerous extracts from Cowpers poems and several of his hymns were also sung during the evening. The Rev. J. Kirtlan proposed and Mr. W. Silcock seconded a vote of thanks to the lecturer, which was passed unanimously, and the meeting closed with the pronouncing of the benediction. LEGH-STREET PRIMITIVE METHODISTS. The Sunday School sermons were hell at the Primi- tive Methodist Chapel, Leg.h-steon Sunday,the preacher morning and evening being the Rev. W. Graham, of Golborne (formerly in the Warrington Circuit). In the afternoon an address was given by Mr. F. R. Roberts, M.A., of Warrington. Special hymns were song by the children in a very creditable manner, under the leader- ship of Mr. Podmore, and Mr. James occupied the posi- tion of organist. I here were very large congregations, and the collections were also very good.

T. JOHN'S PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH. The anniversary services in connection with St. John's Presbyterian Church were held on Sunday. The preacher for the day was the Rev. Wilson Cowie, of flowd.)11. The rev. gentleman took as his text in the morning the last chapter of Deuteronomy, 10 verse : "Your eyes shall see it but you will not be allowed to enter therein." His subject for the evening was " Patience." There were good congregations, that in the evening being very large. The cost to the Corporation of Hull for the services of extra police during, the late strike at the docks will amount to about £9,000. This is exclusive of the cost of the military, which may also have to be paid for.

A'I'URDAY, JUNE

ANNUAL MEETING OF WIDNES FOOTBALL CLUB.

The annual meeting of the Widnes Football Club was held on Tuesday evening at the Conservative Assembly Room. There was a large attendance of members present. Mr. J. H. Smith was appointed chairman. A letter was read from Mr. 11. Wade Deacon regretting his inability to attend the meet- ing, in his capacity as president. TUE ACCOUNTS. The balance sheet showed that the total receipts for the year was £486 13s. id. The expenditure including £76 Is. 101. paid to the deficit from last year, amounted to £486 13s. id. including a balance in hand of .£2B 3s. 111 d. The accounts were adopted.

ANNUAL REPORT. The following is the report of the year's work, sub- mitted by the hen. sec., and adopted by the meeting: "The results of the past season, whilst not altogether satisfactory as regards the number of matches won, are, nevertheless, slightly more favturable than the results of the previous season; and as it is of the utmost im- portance—in view of the League matches—that this improvement should continue next season, we trust that the players and all concerned will make an effort to lift the club to a higher position. The "A " team results are most disappointing, only three matches being won during the season. Financially, the season just ended has been one of the best we have had for a number of years. At Christmas the Committee arranged a draw- ing for prizes, which turned out a great success and en- riched the funds of the club to the extent of £3B. We were also particularly fortunate in not losing a single first team gate owing to the weather, while on three occasions we were excused from sending a team away owing te our opponents' grounds being frost-bound, and as one of these was the long journey to Barrow, a con- siderable saving was effected. At the request of the United Alkali Co.,—who generously provided gold medals for the winning team—the Inter-Works Com- petition was resumed after a lapse of several years. This also proved an unqualified success, and the proceeds from the competition together with the profits from the Christmas drawing, were the chief means whereby we are new able to show a credit balance. Since our last report,the Committee have made arrangements with the Cricket Club, to have the use of the ground from Sep- tember Ist to April 30th inclusive, thus enabling us to extend our season to the full limit allowed by the Rugby Union. In conclusion, we beg to thank our members, friends, and spectators generally, for their continued support.

THE COUNTY ASYLUM. Mr. Alex Flornby, clerk of the County Asylum, Chester, wrote pointing out that there had been a trans- ference of patients from that institution to Parkside Asylum, and explaining that the inconvenience of the Union patients being at Micclesfiskl was only tem- porary, as plans for an extension of the asylum to pro- vide accomodation for 400 additional patients were now with the Lunacy Commissioners for their approval and it was expected the work would he commenced in a few months time. The Runcorn Guardians might rely upon the Asylum Committee keeping as far as possible all patients from Unions on the Chester side of the county in their own Institution.—ln a later letter Mr. Flornby states that there was only one bed vacant on the female side and ten on the male side of the Asylum.—The reply was considered very satisfactory, and the Chairman re- marked that the Union was well represented on the Asylum Committee by Mr. P. Speakman. FREE EDUCATION IN RUNCORN. The Rev. S 1 RANOE FREA.K OF A GOLBORNE LADY.

DISCOVERED AT NOTTINGHAM.

Mrs. Maud Ann Johnson, the prepossessing young married lady who mysteriously left her home at Gol- borne last week, and has been extensively advertised for by the police, on Wednesday night communicated with her husband from Nottingham, and he immediately started to fetch her home. She left Golborne presumably for Wigan on Wednesday week, hut turned up at Liver- pool, and in a rambling letter to her husband, giving no address, talked of making away with herself. The hus- band informed the police that until her disappearance their ten months of matrimonial life had been happily spent. The circumstances of her departure and subse- quent movements have been more suggestive of romance than real life.

SCARCITY OF WATER AT BARROW.

The supply of water ia the Barrow reservoirs is be- coming alarmingly reduced, there being at most only nine weeks' supply. The recent rains have made no appreciable difference, The Gas and Water Committee are about to issue handbills urging consumers to econo- mise, and giving notice of a restriction in the supply. A new reservoir is to be constructed, but many months must elapse before it is completed.

WARRINGTON BOAItD OF GUARDIANS

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Town Hall,on Wednes- day. There were present :—Mr. C. Ratcliffe (in the chair), Rev. G. F. Roberts, Dr. Patterson, and Messrs. R. Heaton, Muir, A. Jolley, A. J. Jolley, IV'. J. Shaw, R. Owen, Balharry, Davies, Pilling, W.Gill, William Fairclough, J. Turton, J. Clarke, H.Hi bbert, T. Mather, and James Hilt; Mr. S. Sutton (assistant clerk), Mr. C. H. Ward (master of the house), and Mr. 11. !ines (Ruperintendent of the eChOOlta), THE RECENT FIRE AT THE HOSPITAL.—REFUSING

ASSISTANCE. The minutes of the House Committee stated that on Wednesday last week, an alarm of fire was raised at the Workhouse, and within five minutes after the alarm was sounded, the men had the house engine at work and the hose playing right over the building,. It turned out that the alarm was a false one, and had been raised by two members of tha Committee for the purpose of test- ing the engine.—Mr. DAVIES said that it waz a fact that within live minutes after the alarm the eneine had been put into full working order, which was gratifying, to the Committee. He could not refrain from mentioning a , fact in connection with the recent fire at the hospital. He had always been under the impression that at any ' fire, assistance of any character was always acceptable. He regretted to say that this was not so in respect to the fire he had mentioned. The Workhouse officials sent across to the hospital three minutes after the fire had been discovered and offered the assistance of their men and engine, but to everyone's surprise the proffered assistance was rejected The Workhouse officials were simply told that the Warrington engine had been sent for. It was not for him or anyone to say that the fire could have been put out had their engine been sent to the rescue,but it was exceedingly probable that would have been checked. (Hear, hear.) He considered it was a matter which required looking into. It would make their Committee rather chary at offering assis- tance in quarters where it was treated so lightly. Of course, as the building .had ..been: erected by the rate- payers' money it was probably Dna much consequence. —Mr. Mum said that as a member of the Fire Engine Committee of the Town Council lie could say that there appeared to be some muddle about the affair. Miss Walker, the matron of the hospital, said she did not re- member any offer of assistance being made. He was sure the Corporation were obliged to the Workhouse officials for their offer—if it had been made; hut even supposing they had taken, their enjne to the fire three minutes after it was discovered, it would not have been of any service, as the flames had gained complete hold. —Several Members stated that it was an undoubted fact that the offer of assistance bad been made and refused, and that Mr. Ward, master of the house,had in his possession a letter to that effect from the hospital.— Mr. HEATON pointed out that it was a serious matter to refuse assistance in such a matter. The town engine, when it did come, he said, was only able to work through receiving a water supply from a Workhouse main.—Mr.Oweis asked if the building had been insured, and he was informed that it had not.

DR. WATKINS AND THE WARRINGTON BOARD OF 'GUARDIANS.

At the fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians, held at the Town Hall, on Wednesday, Mr. G. Ratcliffe in the chair, the question of Dr. Watkin's charges tor midwifery fees was brought up again. At the last meeting of the Board, Dr. Watkins sent in a bill of a 9s. for five cases he having charged the special rate of .£2 for four out of the five cases. This had been ccnsiderecl remarkable and the bill had been referred back to the Newton Committee.—Mr. HIBBERT, one of the Newton Committee, expressed his indignation at this and wanted to know why it had been referred back since the account had been properly examined by the Committee and signed by their Chairman, Mr. Balharry.—Mr. W. HOUGHTON said ho understood that the till had been referred back on the proposition of members of the Comm:ttee.—A somewhat lengthy discussion then took place, in the csurse of which Mr. Balharry expressed his regret that such a reflection should be cast upon him- self and the Committee. He had signed the account after fully investigating it, and during the whole of his 28 year's membership on the Board he bad never met with such a regrettable experience.—A proposition was made that the account sh.eild be passed, but Mr. A. J. JOLLEY proposed an amendment that it should not be paused, because he considered the four special cases were most extraordinary and no proper evidence had been given in support of them. —Another discussion followed after which Dr. Pattison read a letter from Dr. Watkins detailing the circumstances of the special cases.—ln the face of this document Mr. Jolley withdrew his amend. merit, and the accounts were passed. •

MR. CHAMBERLAIN IN A CARRIAGE ACCIDENT.

A serious accident occurred to Mr. Joseph Chamber- lain's carriage in Piccadilly, on Wednesday night. The horse slipped, and fell, causing damage to the glass of the vehicle. The right hon. gentleman was inside at the time, but he alighted apparently unhurt.
