BETHESDA CONGREGATIONAL SUNDAY

SCHOOL, RUNCORN.

JOHN BULL IN HIS BEST CLOTHES.

The anniversary of Bethesda Congregational Sun- dap school, was celebrated on Sunday, when the Rev. F. Carter of Northwich, preached morning and evening to large congregations. In the, evening the sac-ed edifice was crowded, At each service the choir and schllars sang specially selected hymns, Mr. Clifford LeCauteur presiding at the organ. The collec- tiens realised nearly £5. On Monday evening the Rev. , Silas K. Hocking, of Southport, delivered his popular lecture" John Bull in his best clothes." There was only a moderate attendance. The lecturer explained that his addresses was in no way political ; it was simply an attempt to give a picture of an ideal Englishman, to show John Bull, not exactly as he WAS, but as he might be at his best and noblest. By foreigners John Bull was always depicted as a wine bibbing, beef eating individ- ual. He was not prepared to defend all John Bull's weaknesses. And if there be any dry ret in the English constitution it was surely to be found in this debasing habit of constant futldling, which Was imparing the national health. which was increasirik their burdens and decreasing their resourees ; which was crippling their industries and ren- dering men by thousands unfit for labour ; which WAS emptying their Churches and Sunday schools, and fill- ing their workhouses and gaols, and which was open- ing the door to hundreds of other vices which had long been clamouring for admittance. (Applause) If any future Gibbon had to write the fall of the British Empire, it would be from "the enemy that was allowed to enter his mouth aud steal away his brains." Might the day be far distant whom it should be written on John Bull's I tombstone that he died through drink. (Applause) It I was said that John Bull was a bragging and grasping I pugilistic bully, and an altogether sanguinary indivi- dual. He denied this and mentioned the advancement made respecting national disputes being settled by arbi- tration, and expressed a hepe that the time would never come when John Bull would unsheath his sword in an unholy cause. England was not behind any other coun- try in respect of labour and enterprise. He had made himself what he was to-day by hard work. His resolute will had eenerally conquered, and this had given him the right to wear his best clothes everyday, and to com- mand the respect of those who were envious of his p)wer. (Applause.) The dress of the typical John Bull was in- dicative of his character, for with all his faults they could not but admire hiin, and entertain the hope that his life might be long and prosperous. His boots, with broad bottoms, thick soles and high tops, denoted deter- mination and strong common sense. His coat showed comfort and convenience rather than elegance. To ray John Bull did not like beauty was untrue, as proved by the beautiful art schools, picture galleries, and public buildings all up and down the country. The lecturer rediculed the aesthetic craze, and said John Bull would not feed on a sunflower while there was a beefstake about, rem sleep on a marble slab if there was a feather bed handy. (Laughter.) With regard to the tall hat, whilst it was uncomfortabla it had the redeeming feature that it Was Capable of an immense amount of polish,and therefore 'night be taken as indicative of his good man- ners. The lecturer considered that John Bull carried his reserve too far, and was too careful about his introthic- tions. This was done in cdnnection with the Chris- tian Church. He advised them toeive a hearty welcome to strangers who might come intontheir midst. All true politeness had its root in due regard for the rights and feelings of others. He cautioned smokers against be- coming too selfish, and annoying others. Tobacco second hand was nasty. John's umbrell whs demonstrative of his charity, which was known througlicut the world. He gave to every cause. No cry of distress or wail of sorrow did he listen to without trying to relieve. Dealing with John Bull at home, Mr. Hocking stated that his dressing gown and slippers were emblematic of his domesticity, and to this trait of his character he owed not a little of his strength and greatness. (Aoulause.) The family was before the nation, for out of the former tha latter grew, and no nation could be great where family ties and relationships were ignored. While their homes remained unstained, and their hearthstones pure, England's glory would not wane, nor her strength pass away. Wite in Eneland meant wife, and in no country Was woman treated with such veneration as in England. This had given John Bull gentleness, dignity, and I chivalry. Dealing with John Bull in his go-to-meeting coat the lecturer said it showed the respect English people had for their Sabbath, and then urged that if they were truly proud ot their couutry let them show is by their honour, pride, chivalry and truth. Let them see to it that no shame fell on the English name. If they loved their country let them be true to it, and do honour to the name they bore. (Applause.) The customary vote of thanks concluded the meet- ing.

MONDAY.

The House of Commons reassembled on Monday after the Whitsuntide recess ad went into Committee of Supply. A vote on account was submitted, and it led to discussion on a large number of subjects—The Ordnance Survey, lighthouse efficiency, State empkvment of old soldiers and sailors, cost of the House of Lords Staff, &c. On the Marquis of CARMARTHEN calling attention to the dangerous and growing practice of carrying revolv- ers, Mr. ASQUITH said the Government were considering the question, and if the House was disposed to treat it in a non-contentious manner they did not despair of effecting improvements in the law. Sir Jons GORST accused the rresident of the Board of Trade of political partiality in the appoirtment of the labour correspondents to the Bard of Trade. Mr. MCrNDELLA repudiated the charge. The vote on account was passed before the House rose.

TUESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Tuesday evening, Mr. MOBLEY replying to Sir T. LEA,stated that circumstances under which the police of Londonderry interfered with the display of flags from the windows of public-houses en the occasion of Lord Salisbury's recent visit. Ho said no order whatever was issued from the central authority' who had no cognisance of the transaction till after the event. Under the law the constabulary might enter any licensed premises and remove or destroy any flag,banner, ' symbol, or decoration, but he did not think the Union 'Jack was one of the flags contemplated in the Act. The motion for adjournment over the Derby-day— proposed by Mr. BuoosviEtn—was defeated by 231 to I 169.

The House again went into Committes on the Home Rule Bill, and proceeded to consider Captain NAYLOR.. LEYLAND'S amendment that clause 3be postponed. On a division the amendment was rejected by 273 to 240. Lord WOLSIER moved an amendment to the clause to the effect that the Irish Legislature should not have power, not only to make laws, but to pass resolutions in re- ference to the matters taken from them by the clause. Mr. GLADSTONE said they had no means of enforcing such a condition. Mr. BALFOUR. said this argument de- molished the whole of the Prime Minister's case. He asked what were the intentions of the Government as to the Irish members. Mr. GrAnsroNE said they intended to do their best to induce the House to adopt the 9th clause. Mr. Cuamaiumetx said the debate had elicited a most satisfactory admission from the Prime Minister. The amendment was then defeated by 259 to 238. Pro- gress was then reported. WLDNESDAY. Proceedings in Committee on the Home Rule Bill were continued in the House of Commons on Wednesday. Viscount WOLHER moved an amendment to clause 3. providing that the Irish Parliament should not be able to entertain or getnt votes in supply except on the re- commendation of the Crown, signified by a Minister of tke Imperial Government. Mr. GLAnsroxs opposed the amendment. He th,,ught that what was necessary had been provided for in the bill, but if it was not quite plain the insertion of a few words in the proper place, clause 10, woult make it absolutely clear. After a long discussion the amendment was rejected by 240 to 188. General GOLDSWORTHY moved an amendment abolishing the office of Lord Lieutenant. Mr. GLADSTONE and Mr- Morley opposed and Mr. Balfour supported the amend- ment, which on a division was defeated by 285 to 219. Sir A. ScOBLE moved an amendment to the effect that the Irish Legislature should do nothing that might affect the authority of Parliament or any part of the unwritten laws or constitution of the United Kingdom whereon might depend the allegiance of any person to the Crown of the United Kingdom. The amendment was lost by 278 to 238. Another amendment, proposing to insert words to prevent the Irish Legislature altering its title. was defeated by 278 to 234. -

THURSDAY. In the House of Commons on Thursday evening, Sir E. GRRY replying to Mr. Labouchere, said that no in- formation of any kind had been receird by her Majesty's Government from Uganda since Sir G. Portal arrived there. Sir W. HARCOURT informed Mr. Chaplain that it was necessary to obtain the opinion of the Indian Govern- ment upon the report of the Committee on Indian Cur- rency, but no time would be lost by either the Indian Government or her Majesty's Government in coming to a decision. Mr. ARNOLD-FORSTER moved the adjournment of the House in order to call attention to the growth of clime in Ireland and the failure of the Executive to take steps for its abatement. Mr. Murmur contended that agrarian crime had decreased since the present Government came into office, though he admitted that in certain parts there had been an increase in offences classed as " moonlighting." rhe increase, however, was only ' temporary, and the police were working as faithfully and as energetically as they did under the late Govern- ment. After a long debate the motion for adjournment was defeated by 241 to 203—maj )riry for the Govern- ment 38.

SHIP CANAL.

THE BARTON AQUEDUCT ACCIDENT. An accident occurred on Tuesday in connection with tho deviation of the Bridgewater Canal at Barton. The swing aqueduct which is to take the place of Itrindley'a famous structure was completed Same few days ago. It has a northern and a southern approach, the ends of which are separated from the Bridgewater Cantl by dams. Portions of the dams were removed on Tuesday and the water entered the new channel. Unfortunately the bed proved to be infirm, and the water flowed through and flooded the adjoining Isnd, eventually finding a way into the Ship Canal. The damage done to the deviation works will cause some delay.

THE LONDON AND NORTH-WESTERN RAILWAY' , COMPANY AND THE CANAL. An important arbitration between the London and North-Western Railway Company and the Manchester Ship Canal Company was opened in London on Tuesday. The Canal Company, as is well known, have been obliged to construct" deviation" works on the North- Western to can, the railway on new lines over the iwaterway. The approaches to the new bridges are necessarily long and the gradient.; heavy, and the Canal Company is under statutory bonds to compensate the North-Western and other companies for the increased cost in the maintenance of way and the haulage of trains which the deviations will bring about. Mr. Pope, in stating the case for the Railway Company, said that his clients were desirous of hastening rather than of deferring by any action in regard to compensation the opening of the canal for traffic. Mr.Pember,for the Canal Company, thereupon asked the learned counsel to state what sum his clients thought would ba required, and promised that the Canal Company would, if it were possible, de- posit it at once—subject to the award of the arbitrator at a future day,—in order that the opening of the canai might not be delayed. The railway companies, it seems, have the power to retain p ,ssession of the ground which the Canal Company requires until the award of the arbitrator shall have been paid. Mr. Pope pro- mised to ascertain the amount " in round figures," and after some evidence had been given the proceedings were adjourned. The arbitration upon the claims of the London and North-Western and Great Western Railway Companies against the Manchester Ship Canal Company was con- tinued on Wednesday at Westminster, before Lord Balfour of Burleigh and SirG. Bruce. At the commence- ment of the day's proceedings Mr. Pope is formed Mr. Pember that the claimants were not then prepared to state the sum they would require to be deposited before the canal company could get possession of the old lined in Warrington and the vicinity. It was a matter for negotiation, and had nothing to do with the arbitration. —Mr. Harrison, the general manager of the London and North-Western, was examined, and it transpired in the course of his evidence that the aggre.rate of his cons- pany's claims by reason of deViation of lines and addi- tional cost of working was about- 330,003. The devia- tions had made no difference in their system of working. In cross-examination he said he would not admit that many of their trains were habitually behind time.—Mr. Pope : We make ir) claim for waht of punctuality.—Mr. Hat-Aeon further stated that a p Irticular train through from Carlisle to Stafford, and which passed over the deviation at Warrington, was nonctual, but there was time lost at Warrington owing to the signals, and that was made up on the compsny's old lines.—Mr. George Whale, examined by Mr. Pop 3 said he was assistant locomotive superintendent of the London and Nofth- Western, and was stationed at Crewe. He had been upwards of thirty-four years in the service, and sixteen THheeerxepolaisneadtotthale yweoarrksi na as loocr the ottri avffie c s ITe w r ianrtrei nn 4d et onnt : tonnage there in the course of the year of 2 918,937 tons or an average of 1821 tons per t.-tin, which vro:.nd have to go up a gradient of one in 135 on the main line.—The inquiry was adjourned.

THE SHIP CANAL ANp.ITS PROSPECTS.

A London correspondent writing to a Manchester con- temporary, says :—ln conversation with the representa- tive in London of one of the foremost fleets of steamers trading between Gre!it Britain and the Eaet, I sounded him as to the intentions of his line in relation to the Manchester Ship Canal. At present he eets a cargo from Yorkshire, Lancashire, and the Midlands almost indiscrimately at Leaden or Liverpool, the extra car- riage to the South being set off by shippers against a saving in time. He thought it unlikely that his boats, which are tremendous twist screws of the latest type, would proceed to the furthest limit of the canal. nut he is already thinking over a scheme for sending a steam tender up to Manchester to bring down not only cotton bales, but also general cargo trom the interior, which he hopes it will be possible to get to the quayside at Manchester at lower rates than it gets direct to the mouth of the Mersey at present. Should his hopes be realised he thinks he will he able to tap a good deal of non• Lancashire traffic, which at present might or might not find its way to the Thames.

SHIP CANAL RAILWAY DEVIATION. OPENING OF CADISHEAD STATION. On Thursday morning, the new station at Cadishead, on the Ship Canal Deviation Railway of the Cheshire Lines was opened. The station is situated between Stockport and Warrington. The old station, which can still be seen in the vicinity, hai been closed for several years, owing to the fact that it was not convenient. Main Station, three quarters of a Mile distant, offered greater facilities for travelling to AlAnchestar. DurinF the years that it has been closed the population of Caclishead has greatly increased. The railway devia- tion, on which the station stanch, =tends on either side of the Canal for over a mile, and running parallel with the old line, but with a far steeper gradient. The old line will not altogether be discarded. It will be used to carry coal to the Partington coal basin from the Wigan and Yorkshire coalfields.

was suffocated by its parents, a regrettable feature When it is remembered that such deaths are nearly always the result of either the carelessness, the intemperance, or even the crime of the parent. hi looking out for remedies or preventives of excessive infantile mortality it is curious to note how little the death-rate of children is influenced by sanitary improvements. In Birmingham Improved sanitation has lessened the general death-rate by something like twelve per cent., yet there has been no reduction in that of the chil- dren ; and, generally speaking, the same peculiar- ity has been observable throughout the country. 14°st people will, therefore, agree with Dr. HILL, When he says that social rather than sanitary agencies must he looked to for effecting the desired reduction in infantile mortality. Dr. HILL is, too, aPParently pretty much of the same opinion with regard to child insurance as Mr. JUSTICE WILLS, Whose remarks upon that subject we commented uPon recently. According to the Medical Officer of Birmingham the regulation of infant life insur- ance might be undertaken with advantage to Infant health and life.

Significant Mr. GLADSTONE returned to Lon- a Deciaration. don from Hawarden on Monday, and in a brief speech which he raade at Chester Station he used some rather not- "le words. After remarking that it would be better to reserve for further opportunities what he had to say with regard to the nature of the oPposition in Parliament, he added : "There may be an improvement ; but whether there is an majority, illTrovernent or not, a majority after all is a and I think you will find that there is nn spirit whideficiency in the masculine and determined ch is, at all events, characteristic of our °°untrynien, and that the res,ilt will be such as :on desire." Now, what those Mr. GLADSTONE 11 addressing most ardently desired was that he nould in_sist upon respect for the constitutionally ex.. pressed will of the country in the face of obstruc- tal°n and threats, and if his words,cautious as they re, mean that he is resolved to meet with a do- COWont the flagrant obstruction that is iv . ranapant in the House of Commons they ill awaken a responsive chord in the breast of every Liberal in the country. For there is now ° getting b the 14 eyond the fact that, in regard r --°rne Rule Bill, the Government is face to Isittee with undisguis.A and undiluted obstruction. °n° of the amendments that are proposed and of the discussions that take place are for the Vrpose of improving or altering the Bill. The cinrY leaders, from the Marquis of SALISBURY ,°wllwards, have openly avowed that they will not ave the Bill in any shape or form, and 110 secret la.. made of the intention to use their majority in ho House of Lords to throw out the Bill, no matter in what guise it may be "sent up." For Nhat object, then, aro these innumerable amend- ments concocted, and these endless discussions f,rtted on ? 'lYaste time,Clearly, for no other purpose than and to delay the other reforms that aro on atruction, the Guvernment programme. This is ob- pure and simple. WOMAN SERIOUSLY BURNED.

About ten o'clock on Wednesday night, an elderly Widow named Forshaw, residing at 63, St. Helens. was seriously burned by the upsetting of a paraffin lamp. She was carrying the lamp up- stairs, when it caught against a door at the top lind fell from her hand. The oil quickly ignited and set fire to her clothing, while the blazing oil an down the stairs and into the room. The woman rushed out into thd street, where a couple of men "PDressed the flames arising from her clothing, %hile several neighbours extinguished the fire on the stairs and in the room Dr. Fred Knowles was 19:11uediately summaned to the injured woman, and he found her so terribly burned about the body that he Ordered her removal to the Providence Hospital. The Post officials of Runcorn, removed from their old dark and inconvenient office in High-st., orf Thurs- day, into the new premises prepared for them in Vic- toria Buildings, Savages Bridge. The new offices are handsome, commodious, well finished, capitally arranged and well ventilated. There is, every convenience for both the public and the officials in all departments of postal work. The office is part of an imposing pile of buildings which are an ornament to the town, con- structed by Messrs. Farrell and Son, for Mrs. S. E. Speakman, wife of Mr. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C., Mr. Rimmer is the architect. In a few days the Williams, Deakin and Manchester and Salford Bank will occupy handsome offices in the buildings. PRITCHARD'S have now stood the test for fifty years, and quality and first-class workmanship are still guaranteed.— Advt. HEROIC DEED BY A SIGNALMAN AT RUNCORN. About half-past seven on Thursday evening, George Woods, the signalman at Ditton Bank box,between Dit- ton and Runcorn, observed a man walking in the direction of Runcorn Bridge, in the fourfoot of the down line to Liverpool. An express goods train was coining down, but the man continued on his way wildly waving his bands apparently to stop the train. On came the train hut the man made no effort to get away and his death would have been certain when Woods ran to him picked him up, and carried him out of danger, just as the train dashed past. The man was given into the custody of the police, to whom he described himself as a labourer named Jonn Smith,of Granville-at., Runcorn, He was brought before the Mayor, at Widnes, on a charge of being a wandering lunatic, and he was com- mitted to the Workhouse.

If you want your WATCH REPAIRED by skilled workmen take it tO PRITCHARD'S.—Adrt BRUNSWICK IMPROVEMENT CLASS, RUNCORN RAMBLING CLUB.

The second outing in co-n-si-e-ction with this club took place on Saturday. The place selected was Dunadale, Overton, the seat of Mr. John Simpson, senior Superin- tendent of the school. The party numbered 50, and were conveyed in brakes, provided by Mr. Thos. Booth, Church-st. On their arrival they were received by Mr. 1 Simpson, who expressed the pleasure it gave bim to meet them. He 'conducted them round the grounds, and pointed out to the party many objects of interest. The party then had tea on the lawn in picnic style. Miss Spearing, of Church-st., catered for the party in admir- able style. After tea, Mr. D. Bisbrown moved a resolu- tion thanking Mr-Simpson for his kindness. In doing so he said that they were glad to offer a few words of kindly appreciation to Mr. Simpson as he was always to the fore in every good work. The proposition was seconded by Mr. J. H. Darlington, secretary of the class, it was supported by Mr. John Birkett, and carried with acclamation. Mr. Simpson replied in suitable terms. After this the group were photlgraphed by Mr. James Pickthall, a member of the committee. A numoer of pieces were sung, and games were indulged in. The party returned, arriving at Runcorn at 9.30, having had a most enjoyable out. Go to PRITCHARD'a, 51. nigh-street. Runcorn, for the Lest FIVE GUINEA. WATCH a workingman can have.—Adv 5. FIEENVELL OF THE Rev. R. MEDILEJOHN OF WIDNES. The Rev. R. Meiklejohn, senior "curate of Widnes parish, preached his farewell sermons on Sunday, pre- vious to commencing his duties at Hastings. He occu- pied the pulpit at the Parish Church in the morning, and in the evening at St. Paul's Church. He said if any of them had received benefit from his ministry thcri it had not been in vain.—On Monday evening a meeting of the St. Paul's Bible Class took place, when on behalf of the members,Councillor F. Neil presented Mr. Meikle- john with a handsome gold watch, hearing the follow- ing inscription, " Presented to the Rev. 11. Meiklejohn, by the members of his Bible Class and other friends as a token of their esteem, May, 1893."—Councillor Neil said Mr. Meiklejohn's kind words and sympathy have been the means of cheering and comforting many a heart,and the active interest he had taken in the sports of the youth of the town had had an elevating influence. In fact his influence had been felt throughout the parish.— Remarks of an appreciative character were also made by Messrs. F. Smith and J. C. Crawford.—Mr. J. Bingham, on behalf of the Women's Bible Class, presented Mr. .Meiklejohn with a silver mounted walking stick. In acknowledging the gifts, Mr. Meiklejohn, thanked the members for their kindness to him and said he was sorry to leave Widnes, but it had been arranged that he was to go and he would have to do so. ACCIDENT TO A LURRY3IA.Y.

On Tuesday evening, Alfred Sudlow, second son of Mr. Wm. Sadlow, met with a painful accident while following his occupation as a lurryman at Messrs. Rylands' wireworks, Church-street, War- rington. He was engaged loading, when a bag of staples, which was sbout to be lowered, fell on him as he was standing on the lurry. He was struck on the head and shoulders, and rendered uncon- scious. On being taken home he remained in an unconscious condition for three hours, but after- wards passed a good night.

THESGS HEARD AND SEEN AT ItUNCORN.

BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

After hearing Mr. Hocking's lecture on " John Bull in his best clothes" at Bethesda Church on Monday evening, an occasional correspondent eased bia soul of the following lines. All glory to me, John Bull, Of pride I am always full And iniQhry swag. None other 'fore me can stand, For I'm first 4, every land, On bleak or on sunny straw], God bless my brag I

That was a capital description of the modern drawing room. meant to be looked upon but not used. Tlki table legs in trousers, the piano in a pinafore, the chairs in chintz, and a stretcher on the carpet.

Through the death of Mr. Henry Coward, the Board of Commissioners loses the assistance of a practical and farseeing man, and the regret of the mem hers is expressed elsewhere. Mr. Coward was a man of strict, integrity and honour, and he will he much missed in the sphere in which he moved. The funersi on WednesdAy was a tnitgnificont demon- stratiou to his eiiariteti,ir worth.

There were five deaths from smallpox in Run- corn during the past month, but I am in tornird that the disease is again on the decline. Dr. Mcamigall's statement that the disease is not due to any un- sanitary conditions will be reassuring, and Mr. Wiswall's description of the Rustling of the sewer and syphon will set at rest the rumouts that have been afloat. '

Mr. Humphreys seems to be pursued by an un- kind fate. Nearly the whole of his proposals to the Commissioners seem to be entirely rejected or have an untimely end. His idea with regard to dividing the town into wards was well worth carry- out, and the members openly approved of the prin- ciple, but all the same " chucked 'the motion out. What a strange and wonderful difference there is between principle and practice. As Mr. Humphreys pointed out it would be well if the members carried their pious opimions into action. Mr. Taylor would appear to have lost all faith in the County Council and therefore abstained from voting He declined, I infer to be a party to sending a proposal which judging by the past actions of the County Council would be unceremoniously rejected.

The annual meeting ef Runcorn Commissioners should be held in the course of a few weeks, and the Chairman appointed. Who is to be Mr. Wis- wall's successor ?

Congratulations to Mr. ,and Mrs. Philip Ernest Speakman and hearty wishes for a happy and pros- perous life.

DEATH AND FUNERAL OF MR. COWARD

OF ItUNCOItN.

There was interred in Runcorn Cemetery, on Wednesday afternoon,wit h all the pomp of military display, the mortal remains of Mr. Henry Coward, builder and codtractor, and licensed victualler. For several months Mr. Coward had suffered from a painful illness, and notwithstanding that Dr. McDougall (who called Dr. Glynn of Liverpool into con- sultation) could do, he expired on Saturday night. A native of Overton Marlborough, Wilts, Mr. Coward ' came to Runcorn 30 years ago, and was engaged as a ' joiner by Mr. Henry White, builder. A smart and capable workman he rapidly acquired the position of foreman, which position he held until ten years ago, when he commenced business on his own account, and he had since carried out several large contracts. For nearly a quarter of a century he had been a licensed victualler, keeping first the Duke of Wellington, in Per- cival-lane, and for the last twelve years the Derby Arms Hotel, Top Locks, where he breathed his last. Mr. Coward wasintimately associated with the Volunteer movement. He became a member of the local corps in 1804, in which he attained the rank of colour-sergeant. Two years ago he retired, hut was allowed the honour- able privilege of wearing his uniform, and had he lived until the Queen's birthday it is considered highly prob- able that he would have been further honoured. In his younger days Mr. Coward was an excellent shot,several silver cups testifying to his skill, and he was also an expert swordsman. For two years Mr. Coward had been a member of the Board of Commissioners, where his sound common sease and practical experience as a builder had been of the greatest value in camniittee work. In religion he was a Churchman and in politics a Conservative but those who differed from him both politically and socially, unite in testifying to the worth of the departed gentleman. He was straightforward, upright and honourable in all his dealings, and his generous disposition led him to do many acts of kindness, and charity in an unostentatious manner. He leaves a widow two sons and one daughter, as well as a stepson and a step-daughter to mourn his loss.• He had only attained the age of 53 yeara. The funeral on Wednesday afternoon was a fitting testimony to his worth. In the procession were repre= sentatives from the Board of Commissioners, the Licensed Victuallers' Association, the Oddfellows, the Volunteers, and many tradesmen, and friends were also present. The cortege was of an imposing description and. was viewed by large crowds of people who lined the road- ways up to the cemetery. The Commissioners present were :—F. Wiswall (chairman), H. J. Grice, W. Fisher, T. Lea, D. Bisbrown, W. Leach, R. Abel, Dr. Mangan, S. Taylor, J. B. Holland (treasurer), F. A. Lake (law- clerk ), E. Marshall (secretary), M. Barker (surveyor), J. Wilding (assistant surveyor), J. Percival (sanitary inspector), W. Garratt (market superintendent), and W. Sutton (assistant overseer), and W. Mellor, and H. White (ex-members of the Board). A MINER, SATUE

MARRIAGE OF MR. ERNEST SPEAKMAN, OF RUNCORN.

A marriage of which Runcornians evinced great interest took place at Holy Trinity Parish Church, Twickenham, on Thursday afternoon. The union was between Al 18S Edith Laura, only daughter of the late Mr. Alfred Charles Stearns and Mrs. Stearns of Radnor House, Twickenham and Mr. Philip Ernest Speakman, second son of Mr. Philip Speakman, J.P., C.C., of the Mount, Higher Runcorn. While the family of Speakman is universally known in this part of Ches. hire, the steams enjoy a similar popularity in their own neighbourhood, and long before the arrival of the wed- ding party, the church was filled to its utmost capacity. The interval was agreeably filled by Mr. C. E. Jolley, who played upon the organ, music appropriate to the occasion. The numerous and distinguished guests having taken their places the bridal party arrived. The bride, who was charmingly attired in a white satin dress trimmed with Brussels lace, and tulle veil, was escorted by her uncle Mr. Arthur Stearns, who gave her away. In attendance were six bridesmaids, Miss Eva Stearns, MissSpeakman, Miss A. Drabble, Miss Sybil Bristowe, Miss Constance Thorpe. and Miss Jessie Stearns, and these young ladies were dressed in white silk costumes, and white lace hats with white feathers. Adorning each of their necks was a half moon pearl brooch, with pearl star, the gift of the bridsgroorn, and in their hands they carried lovely bouquets of Marechal Neil roses. Mr. Thos. W. Speakman, brother of the bridegroom, acted as beat man. The ceremony was performed by the Rev. David Anderson, rector of St. George's, Han- over Square, assisted by the Rev. P. B. Drabble, vicar of Holy Trinity Parish, Twickeaham. The service was fully choral, the choir singing during the progress of the interesting event " The voice that breathed o'er Eden and " 0 Perfect love all human thought transcending." The usual formalities having been observed in the yes- try,the organ pealed forth Mendelsslin'a Wedding March as the bridal party left the sacred edifice. Subsequently Mrs. Stearns held a reception at Radnor House, and the presents many of which were of unusual value, and beauty, were inspected with interest. Mr. and Mrs. Ernest Speakman were overwhelmed with congratula- tions of their numerous friends and in the course of the afternoon departed for Scotland where the honeymoon will be spent. In Runcorn, Widnes, and Sutton Weaver where Mr. Speakman is well known flags arid bunting were flying in honour of the event.

In the evening, the firm of Messrs. Speakman enter- tained the staff to dinner at Wilson's Hotel, Mr. C. Dutton, the proprietor, putting a capital repast before the guests. Mr. William Carroll presided, and after the cloth had been cleared, expressed regret that no member of the firm was present, but it was the wish of Mr. Speakman that they should thoroughly enjoy themselves on that auspicious occasion. The toast "'rho Queen" having been duly honoured, the Chairman proposed the health of the bride and bridegroom. He did so, he said, with very great sa' isfaction because the toast included the health of one whom they all not only esteeinecl, but loved—Mr. Earnest Speakman. (Applause.) In Mr. Ernest they had a thorough English gentleman—a worthy son of a w‘rthy sire, and a geetlernan who at all times was very thoughtful of the welfare and well being of all with whom he was connected. (Applause.) lie (the Chairman) hoped that the union that had taken place that day would be one that would bring to both bride and bridegroom nothing only joy and happiness and that their lives would le both prolonged and pros- perous, such as would add joy to Mr. Speakman's, (the father's) declining years. Lie was sure that in using this language he was only echoing tlw feelings of all present, and that they would heartily drink the toast.—The toast was drunk with musical lismours.—Mr. W. Neild gave the sentiment " Our Employers," and commented upon the fair and generous manner in which they dealt with their servants. The toast was drunk with enthusiasm and the singing of " For they are jolly good fellows."—The Chairman said it would give him great pleasure to intimate to the firm the kind words they had given expression to through Mr. Mild, and he could assure them that their sentiments would be heartily re- eiprocated.—Mr. Clarke the oldest servant of Messrs. Speakman proposed " Prosperity to the Firm," which was duly toasted, and the health of the chairman, the manager of the firm, was drunk with musical honours. Other toasts were also given. The pleasure of those ' present WAS enhanced by ,pianot.rte solos played by Mr. J. Temperley, and by songs given by Messrs. W. Neild, A. Riley, J. Chamberlain, John Turner, F. Leath- wood and Parker. A most enjoyable eNeling was brought to a close by singing the National Anthem. Yesterday (Friday) Mrs. Stearns entertained at Radnor House the members of the local habitaion of the Prim- rose League, of which she is the ruling councillor, the members of the Twickenham Local Board, and represen- tatives or the various branches of the public service. The lawns, which slope gently down to the Thames and adjoin those of Pope's Villa, the residence of Mr. Labouchere, were prettily illuminated, and the guests were permitted to inspect the many valuable wedding presents, tastefully arranged together. The following is a list of the presents :—Set of Honi- 'ton lace, d'oyleys, oak piano and stool, gilt table, two oil paintings (by Holland), old China vase, Mrs. Alfred Stearns ; case of cutlery, silver mounted water jug, glasses,and spirit decanter,Mr.W.A.Stearns; six silver salt cellars ; six silver salt cellars and spoons, Mr. and Mrs. Arthur Stearns; silver pepperettes (Pairt),Miss Eva and Miss Jeseie Stearns ; pair of fish carvers, Messrs. Arthur and E. C. Stearns ; mounted photographer, Mr. Arthur Stearns. jun. ; silver tea and coffee I service, and tray, Mr. J. G. Chillingworth ; I case of fish knives and forks, Mrs. J. G. Chillingworth ; pair of silver candlesticks, Dr. and Mrs. Bristoloe ; case of table silver, twelve Egyptian finger bowls, Mr. T. IV. ST. JON AMBULANCE ASSOCIATION,

RUNCORN BRANCH.

An examination was conducted by Dr. W. Lees, of Chester, in connection with the local branch Of the above association, on May 18th, and a most favourable report has been received by the hon. secretary, Mr. W. Epps, which reflects great credit upon the hon. lecturer, Dr. McDougall. In the ladies' class 15 presented themselves for examination, of whom 13 were successful ; while in connection with the male classes, 23 were examined for the first time, 11 for the second, and 10 for the third and final examin- ation. Of these 20 passed the first, while the whole of those attending tor the second and third times were suc- cessful. FRODSHAM WATER SUPPLT.—The Clerk (Colonel Ashton) has reported to toe Frocisham Parochial Committees that he had called at the offices of the Local Government Board with reference to the proposed inquiry which is to be held with regard to the Frodsham water supply, when he was informed that as soon as the Government Inspector for the district Was at liberty he would arrange to hold the inquiry. A long discussion ens Jed, and it was resolved that the Clerk write to the Local Govern- ment Board pressing them to fix the date of the inquiry as the water supply to the inhabitants of Frodshain is most urgent. A supplementary report was read from Dr. J. Carter Bell, with regard to the recent analysis of water from the borehole, from which it appeared that he was of opinion the water had no action upon lead, and was, therefore, a very good class of water. LY MM.

-- RETURN OF TRH VOLUNTERRS.—About half-past four on Saturday afternoon, the Ly mm Volunteers returned home from their week's sojourn at Conway camp for the annual training. The corps, which numbered about 100 strong, marched throJgh the streets headed by the regi- mental band. -

A COTTAGE ON FIRE.—At about half-past eight on Sun- day morning, smoke was seen issuing out of the win- dows of a cottage in Rush-green, Lvmm, in the occuoa- tion of a labourer named John White. Upon the out- break being discovered, the door of the house was knocked loudly at in order to rouse the inmates, but no response being given, the alarm was conveyed to a policeman, who was in the neighbourhood,and he imme- diately effected an entrance into the house, which was full of blinding smoke. The fire was located in the first floor hedroom,where the floor was blazing. With the assistance of people who had gathered around the house the extent of the fire was lessened, :and the brigade, which had arrived while the police were labouring indus- triously, succeeded In entirely extinguishing the flames shortly after their arrival. Tho tenants of the house, it appeared, left home on Sp.turdav tot a holiday trip, and ! how the fire has occurred is at p-reont a mystery. The only explanation that is ;it preseu4 offered is based on I.h.- discovery of two matches which were found lying on the floor close to a heap of hines, which was partly con- sumed, and it is sup Posed that one of the family before leaving the house accidentally kieheit off the head )f lane of the matches, and that a spark alighted among the been, thus cat:sing the been to ignite and smoulder.

RUNCORN BOARD OF COMMISSIONERS.

I BANK QUAY SOAP WORKS SOCIAL

SOCIETY.

week, at Bank Quay. But the preoccupation of busi- ness and the stern necessity for what he had frequently referred to as "Ship discipline," and he could not find a better expression for that graduated authority which indispensible in a concern like theirs—that and exigen- cies of titne put the atnenities of friendly intercourse eg a general rule wholly out of the question. But there, these obstacles were out of the way. They met at their leisure on neutral ground, and it would be a pleasure to him to come to one of their annual mains, were it only as an earnest of good feeling. (Applause.) They need not pretend to be bosom friends. The circumstances he had just mentioned hardly admitted of that. But it was satisfactory to think that capital and labour were not always animated by the spirit of dire hatred, and that spite of occasional difference of opinion, there ought even to exist a certain amount of friendliness between them. He thanked them for a very pleasant evening. (Loud applause.) Mr. Lows proposed as a toast, the firm of Messrs. Crosfield and Sens. This was drunk with much en- thusiasm, and accompanied by three cheers and the singing of " For they are jolly good people." Mr. A. H. CHOSFIBLD, in replying. said he remembered some eight or ten years since, his Uncle George remark- ing that it was seldom a firm held a lc tiding positron in any branch of trade for three or four generations con- secutively. Towards the third generation, they began as a rule, he said to taper off. Well, gentlemen, they did not intend to taper off. (Cheers.) On the contrary, they intended to maintain,and he thought he might add, to extend their connection both at home and abroad. That was the intention of his brother and himself, and lie may add,of the senior of their firm—(applanse).—who had been connected with it, since, he thought, 1845, and all of them must hope would remain so for another I generation. (Applause.) And as regarded that inten- ion, iu spite of all competition, and speculative compe- tition, with which they had to contend—that sort of hit or miss business, which meant a wonderful success here and there, but also serious failure in other cases—in the teeth of all that competition and against business on sounder lines, he believed they should be able to achieve their purpose. (Hear, hear.) Mr. WILL/TT proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. A. H. Crosfiold for his presence with them that night. He was sorry that Mrs. J. J. Crosfield had been unable to stay with them, as she had an engagement that night. He also spoke of the good feeling existing between Messrs. Crosfield and their employes. Mr. T. Itatamve seconded the motion which was carried unanimously. Mr. Cuosrstp briefly replied and then bade theim goad night. A nutnber of songs ware afterwards elven by Messrs. T. Handles, D. Norman, J. Wilcock, E. Percival, R. Sewell, Hayden, Willett, and J. Allison, the last two gentlemen being encored. Mr. A.Long acted as accom- panist. Mr. D. Smith contributed a recitation, and a pleasant meeting concluded about ten o'clock.

The monthly meeting WitB held at the Town Hall on Thursday morning, Mr. F. Wiswall presided, and there were present Messrs. W. Fisher, J. Pritchard, S. Taylor, T. Lea, E. W. Humphreys, W. Hankinson, J. Meadowcroft, D. Bisbrown, IL Abel, Dr. Mangan; F. A. Lake (law clerk), E. Marshall (sec.), F. McDougall (medical officer), Mr. Barker (surveyor).

BOWLING MAIN AT TIIE CEMETERY INN.

MR. A. H. CRO,SFIELD ON GAMES.

A bowling main in connection with the Bank Quay Soap Works Social Society was held at the Cemetery Inn, on Saturday afternoon. A good number of the Soap Works employs were present, and there 'were also present Mr. John Crosfield, Mr. Arthur H. Crosfleld, Mrs. J. J. Crosfleld, Mr. Roseeti ; and the members of the committee,Messrs. J. Lawton, S. Welsby, J. Wiicock, T. Lowe, and Mr. J. Willett, secretary. There were 50 entries and Mr.A.ll.Cresfield presented the prizes to the prize winners. The following were the prize wlnnera..:—Mr. .1. Wilcock, copper kettle : Mr. S. Jones, copper kettle ; Mr. T. Handles, clock ; Mr. J. Handles, clock ; Mr. J. Smith, case of dessert spoons and forks ; Mr. P. Gleave, case of dessert spoons and forks ; Mr. E. Pessol, case of carvers, Mr. J. Jackson, knives and forks; Mr. S. Welsby, case of salts ; Mr. Jarvis Woods, case of spoons ; Mr. P. Hill, knives and forks ; Mr. J. iVillett, set of jugs ; Mr. T. Lowe, tea- -1 pot ; Mr. . Highain, set of jngs ; and Messrs. A. Chal- mers, S. Fullwood, J. Rigby, J. Toft, T. Gleave W. Weave, W. Bandies, W. Rhodes, D. Smith, L. Dobson, J. Unsworth, J. Middleton, each won a pocket knife. After the main was over, a substantial knife and fork tea was partaken of, a social meeting being afterwards held. he rttilri thought hat terßa°tshve7 hardi nl itnhees ot Liar that et ef iar isripcleeefcabtl gsaabilde secretary should again trot him out on the subject of bowls ; tor he knew very well, having had to submit to the ordeal of listening to them,that he had already made two speeches no later than the previous Thursday, on that interesting, but still not absolutely inexhaustible topic. Some of the latter orators had the faculty of be- ing able to put the same thing in a dozen different ways Lnd with a dozen varieties of language, so as to tnake it appear snore or less of a novelty each time. He had not that faculty, and therefore they must riot expect him to produce, as the saying went, a quart out of a pint pot. (Laughter.) To tell the truth,lie did not find,as time went on,that soap making and speech making were very easily COM bined. Another characteristic of latter-day oratory was, indeed, said to be that it was sometimes rather • frothy. But then he did not pretend to be ari orator. And if solt soap was supposed as a rule to go d very well—(laughter)—he could plead the very good excus- of being wholly inexperienced in the manufacture. What they turned out at Bank Quay was of the soundest and hardest quality. (A Voice : " it is the hardest any- way." ) (Laughter, and hear, hear.) Ile was much obliged for the compliment. (Laughter.) Hardness was the first requisite of a serviceable bit of soap. The fact was, gentle.nen, that the detail and policy of business had fora long time past absorbed more and more of one's time and thouehts,equally while at and away from Bank Quay ; so that, given a touch of the golfing fever, In addition there was not much room for anythicg else. As they all knew it was an age of specialities. In saying that, he did not refer to any particular brand turned out by themselves, or o:her rival soap firms, whether it be Imperfection, or" " Moonshine," or " Wrong Sort," or any other sort. (Laughter.) He simply referred to that concentratioe, which became raore and more indis- pensable, in whatever line a. man attempted to fight his way to success. And at any rate in the soap trade they had experienced for a good many years past, quite 10 much of what Mr. tilarlstone, the other day, termed "the rub of emulation," as is good for them. But as he remarked in the same admirafile little speech at Hewer- den, it was not intended that the bow should always be bent. The London" Daily Chronicle" might well say, Mr. Gladstone stood convicted of a fine inconsiatencyf fir if any man kept the bow at full stretch, Mr. Glad- stone himself had done so. But very few of them were able like him to go on unweariedly at high pressure, finding in change of occupation, their only relaxation. They needed after a day's work to turn steam off. (Hear hear.) And that was where bowls came in. He did not know of any other outdoor amusement, which as a comprehensive as well as handy recreation combined at once such convenience and socialibity. 'fake its com- prehensiveness. Every one could play at bowls and get some fun out of it. They could begin at ight and play , till they were eighty He was not a very ancient indivi- dual, but it was over 20 years since he first tried his hand at the game, beginning at the very beginning by carry- ing the looter for them, alien his father and his friends were playing at Lymm. After that, with a pair, of light woods, followed initiation into the mysteries of the art. He en- deavoured, not perhaps wholly without bias, to master that delicate and interesting department of the game, which he might describe as the land question. (Laughter.) But he found that though much depended on that, the question of land was on the whole of less importance than that of length. The same held good of many other games where it would at once occur to them that, given a certain accuracy of direction, which was not FO very difficult to attain, the main thing was to have perfect control of strength and judgment,of pace and length. One needed only mention as instances of that, bowling at cricket, or "putting "at golf, or losing hazards into the middle pocket at billiards. And at bowls there was a certain sensuous pleasure in being able to,so to speak, feel the distance with such accuracy, that you put in a" Jack toucher." Then as to the con- venience of bowls, and the way in which the game accommodated itself to all, including those who, perhaps had not too much to spare for onerous subscriptions to more expensive games—that was obvious to everyone. Equally obvious was its sociability. (Applause.) In that respect,lie really thought the game was pre-eminent. It was not only pleacant in itself, but a first rate excuse for enjoying the fresh air of summer evenings, and a bit oh chat and chaff at the same time, whether with or without the gentle stimulus of a cup or a glass of one's favourite beverage. They would notice he put that rather cautiously. (Laughter.) He knew he was tread- ing on delicate ground, or rather he was anxious not to tread on anyone's corns. (Laughter.) But in claiming are-eminence for the sociability of bowls, he admitted there was one important reservation to he made. There was one serious drawback which it shared in common with many other masculine amusements. It withdrew them, unfortunately, during part of their leisure hours, Irons the society of the gentler sex, with whom their lighter moments might otherwise be Spf nt. Possibly, liusbarias might appreciate the company of their help- mate still tatter after a little absence. (Laughter.) He could not speak from personal experience of the matter, but anyway he didn't see how they were to get out of the difficulty unless they could occasionally manage a game of croquet. We are told that they must prepare for a revival of that old fashioned game, and that reminded him of anotherantediluvian fashion with which they were threatened from those distinguished and mysterious quarters whence such things emanate. Deli- cacy forbade him to refer in such detail to this other fashion, but it had already made its appearance in some distinguished circles in the embryo. In that state, he believed, it was an affair of horsehair, whereas, later on, at the fully developed stage, he imagined though these were secrets hidden from man-kind, that it consisted oh hoops and cages. (A voice; " Crinolities,"and laughter.) To his horror he saw a statement the other day to the effect that they might expect the full maturity and development of the fashion in about twelve months' time.. In making these remarks he had touched on one very common topic of the day, viz., the increasing intensity of what was termed the struggle for existence. In every business and profession, even in every depart- ment of art, men were making the same complaint that things are overdone, and it would set m as if the undoubted increase in the severity of com- petition is in some degree proportionate in England and Germany and the States, to tie constant—and in at least two of these countries—rapid increase of the population. But if that was a not alto- ,gether satisfactory sign of the times, there was another feature that was wholly satisfactory, and perhaps in some way connected with it. Neeessity'was the mother of invention, and nature, or human nature, had the knack of avenging itself for an excessive demand upon its strength or faculty, and Ito they saw healthy and en- couraging developments of the means for ratiooalainufe- ment, not only in England, but in those other countries he had mentioned and elsewhere abroad. Let him give them an example. The game of golf that had victirnised so many of them was already making good • further h than America: and had even got a goad that. He was talking at the office the other day to a gentleman who had lived a geomaansi plaeein China, with a name about asinonuirreta eatanthipi art ninf tht (1.1 e a rfuf(iry.insto I;fislicolin: :1 difficultoae narnsres nrnta,ttoswsr)poanimr,conie, enough to tackle the other day.. EALTII OF TIIP. TOWN: REASSIIEING STATEMENTS. The Medical Officer of Health reported that the num- ber of deaths registered during the past month amounted to 36 r of these 2 were inquesecases ; and of the remain- ing 34 cases, 4 were due to inflammation of the lungs, 3 to bronchitis, sto variola (smallpox), 6 to mesenteric disease. 2to plithisis, 2 to heart disease, 2 to disease of the liver, 2to inflammation of the lining membrane of the brain, and one each to kidney disease, jaundice, blood poisoning, diptheria, laryngitis, bowel inflamma- tion, cancer, and natural decay. The 36 deaths showed an average death-rata for the twelve months ot 21.6 and exclusive of ths inquest cases of 20.4 of the estimated Population. The Surveyor said there were 32 deaths in the corres- ponding month last year and 70 the year previous.— Mr. Bisbrown said there were 26 deaths last month.— Mr. Meadoweroft inquired if there was anything in the sanitary condition of: the town that increased the death rate.—Dr. McDougall said he was not aware of anything of an insanitary character that had contributed to the present death rate.—The Chairman said there was a general impression that the new intercepting sewer and syphon under the ship canal needed flushing and di I not get it. This was a very wrong impression. When the new yard was made at the Sprinch,and the Ellesmere-st. sewer aas extended to Heath-road to convey sewage from that district which had previously gone into the canal, the Ship Canal Company at the same time con- nected the four graving docks there with that sewer ; and every time these graving docks were used ,the water was discharged into the sewer at low water, which was the proper time for flushing the sewer and syphon. The sewer and syphon were flushed out on an average,three times a week. The sewers of the town were now better I flushed and in better condition than ever they were I before.—Dr. McDougall thought that was a most ad-1 mirable method of flushing.—Mr. Meadowcroft pointed ' out that previous to the construction of the Ship Canal, the sewage WAS deposited on the foreshore. At the present time they experienced no such drawleack.—Dr. McDougall added that the sewage from the various riparian owners on the banks of the Mersey now passed on beyond the town instead of fouling the shore.—The subject then dropped.

PROPOSED DIVISION INTO NVSIIDS.

Mr. Wynne Humphreys moved " That this Board, being of opinion that the division of the Runcorn Urban Sanitary District into wards would greatly facilitate the recording of votes in Parliamentary and Local Elections and would be further instrumental in securing increased efficiency in the conduct of local affairs, hereby request the Cheshire County Council to exercise the powers conferred by Sec. 517 of the Local Government Act of 1888, and institute an inquiry as to the advisability of such division and to make such order thereon as may be deemed fit and proper. Mr. Humphreys pointed out the advantage that would arise from tile division of the town into wards, and said this was a matter which the Parish Councils Bill did not touch at all. Ths inquiry would not be an expressive or elaborate affair.—Mr. Bisbrown seconded the motion and remarked that it was self evident that the proposal would be a gnat advantage if adopted, and would lead to a more healthy municipal life.—Mr. Taylor agreed that it would boa good thing, but he had not much hope of obtainihg it from the County Council, judging by the contempt with which the County Council had dealt with a very reason- able request made by the ratepayers of Runcorn and by that Board, he had no hope of this being passed.—The Chairman was in sympathy with the motion, but he doubted whether it would be an advan- tage under the present system of voting. They did nut vote by ballot, and every paper must be examined by the presiding officer and officials. It wasa most difficult thing to do now where the voting was concentrated in one place ; what would it be if the town were divided into wards 2—Could not they wait until they were able to vote by ballot, and then the alteration would come naturally P—Mr. Pritchard objected to being called upon to vote on such short notice and on such meagre infor- mation.—Mr. Meadcwcroft considered a case had not been made out, especially considering that they might have a reconstruction of the Board in many important details when the District and Parish Councils Bill was passed He approved in principle of dividing the town into wards, but deemed the present the present time inopportune.—la the course of his reply Mr. Humphreys said they had got in the habit of too often affirming their principles and pious ()Onions and he should like them to do something practical on the present occasion by hav- ing the courage of their opinions.- -The motion was de- feated by five to three.—Dr. Mangan and Mr. Taylor did not vote.

PENICP.TU WATER SUPPLY.

The SURVEYOR reported th:it on the 15th of May, Messrs. Heaton, Jolley, and W.Houghton with the Clerk, Inspector, and Surveyor went to Penketh to examine the eater supply, where they were met by Messrs. Dignum, Parker, Ditchfield, Foster, and Millington. Several houses were visitect, and the following reports made upon them :—" Elle: houses, River-view : Sup- plied from one well. One ten int had a sample in a glass which was discoloured. She said the water a week ago had made her husband ill." This water was ordered to be analysed. " Five cottages and two houses, Garnett- row : Have not water fit to drink ; well was bored 22 yards deep,and was fitte-I with pipes. The pump draws water direct from the bore hole pipes. This water runs perfectly clear and afterwards discolours in the sett- ling." This was ordered to be analysed. Several other cases were reported where the water was obtained from wells and showed discolouration. The wells all appeared, continue° thz report, to have been sunk wheia the houses were erected. At the Penketh Tannery, Mr. Parker said that if the Authority could induce the War- rington Corporation to carry mains to Penketh it war probable the tannery would itself consume and guaran- tee a consumption of about 5,000 gallons per working day. A letter was subsequently written to the Town Clerk of Warrington, asking unon what terms they would supply Penketh with water. Subsequent corres- pondence which took place, contained a letter trona Mr. Whittle, Town Clerk, stating that the Water Committee had instructed him to state that they would recommend the Town Council to undertake the supply of water to Penketh if the Authority would guarantee a payment of Be per cent. upon the capital outlay. This sum would cover the cost of supply and revenue equal to six per cent. upon the rateable vaiue of the premises supplied, and there would be additional charges made for trade purposes, and this would go in reduction of the amount to be paid by the Rural San!. tary Authority. The annual payment would he £340 and ass4ming that the revenue would be £2OO per annum, the Rural Sanitary Authority would have to make up the difference. During a discussion which took place upon the subject, Mr. R. Heaton who hadexamined the water at Penketh, said that it was disvraeeful. Be had pumped water out of what was considered the best well, and the water had run out like tomato sauce. (Laughter.) It was well water, and he had been unwell for a week after tasting it.—Some objection was after- wards taken to the price demanded by the Corporation, and upon the proposition of Mr. Dignum, it was agreed that an alternative estimate be obtained for a supply of Vyrnwy water Department.

THE ROMANTIC SUICIDE AT

NO tTHAIIPTON:

An inquest was held at Northampton on Saturday, before the Borough Coroner, on the body of Dr. Andrew Aikman, who committed suicide by taking prussic acid in the presence of his friend, the Rev. J. Crossby Roberts, D.D.—Mr. Charles Aikman, the younger brother of the deceased, gave evidence regarding the absence of his brother from Northampton and his return on Thursday night. He said that his parents only de- sired a postponement of the wedding for twelve months. If at the end of that time the deceased desired to marry Miss Barritt, his parents would have put no impediment iu the way.—The Rev. J. C. Roberts deposed that when he first spoke to the deceased, two hours before he took the poison, the deceased said to him, "To tell yeu the truth, Roberts, lam half drunk." He did not seem so, however, though in the conversation they had the de- ceased seemed to get muddled. The deceased was upstairs in hissurgery alone about ten minutes with the glass of whisky and water. When he returned with the whisky and water the conversation was resumed as If nothing had occurred. In about five minutes the doctor got up and said " Roberts, do you know the meaning ot the Scotch word ' fey ' ?" " No," replied Mr. Roberts, and thinking that Dr. Aikman was going to give him some details of a nasty disease he added, " and I don't want to know." " Well," returned the doctor, " you soon will know," put ting the emphasis on the " will " ; and sitting down he immediately drank nearly the whole of the contents of the tumbler. At once he be- gan to breathe heavily and lost consciousness. Not- withstanding all the efforts of Dr. Cropley, who was immediately summoned, he died in about eight minutes. Dr. Cropley said that the post-mortem examination re- vealed the fact that the deceased bad taken about hail' an ounce of prussic acid.—Two letters were read by the Coroner, one addressed to Miss Barritt. This, dated" 1, Salisbury-road, Edinburgh, Sunday morning, , 4.30 a.in," was addressed to " My bonnie darling," and I was couched in most affectionate terms. The writer said : , My eyes are aching for a sight of you. Many people have written Ins trying to prove that you don't really care for me. Oh i ! for God's sake say or write whether you do or not. Write for yourself and say it. Don't think me heartless as I have have heard of your state daily, and have many times wired to Dr. C. (Dr. Crop- lee) asking about you. lam under a promise—solemn ' promise—which you know I never break—not to write to you, but my love has proved stronger than any premise. I must write and tell you that I am weary and long to see yoti once more before Idle, my darling. I am Writing unbeknown to anyone, in ,nry pyjamas and.eloale ; the former are my nightdress, ;the Cloak NOll krit/Vic 1 cannot live 'Without you, . and actordinj to others cannot make you happy isy living with you. Oh ! my darling, whose hair I am continually kissing, let me know if you really love me —Your most loving and distractedly Andrew." The second letter was sent to a Northampton friend on May 12th. , The letter said : "lam in a state of abject mental I collapse. Unaccountable as it may seem to you, I still I )ve her dearly, and could I bring myself to believe that after this she could be happy in being married to me, I would visit her and offer to make her my wife at once." —The jury, after a short consultation, returned a ver- dict of Temporary Insanity, adding that they very much regretted to state that they thought that unnecessary and undue influence was brought to bear upon the de- ceased by his friends--Dr. Aikman's father, and brother James, and Mr. and Mrs. barritt, were present at the inquiry, but none oh them were called.—Miss Barritt, who is still confined to her house from the effects of the poison she took on May Bth, has not yet keen informed of her lover's decease.

NEW CATHOLIC SCHOOL-CHAPEL FOR ST. HELENS.

On Sunday morning, the new Catholic school- chapel, dedicated to St. Thomas of Canterbury, at Windleshaw, St. Helens, was opened by the Roman Catholic Bishop of Liverpool (Dr. O'Reilly). The building has been erected by the Catholics of St. Helens and Birchley, to meet the wants of Catholics in a rapidly-increasing district. It con- sists of two storeys, the lower portion being fitted up as a schoolroom, and the upper portion as chapel, but it is so arranged that at some future date, when the growth of the district warrants the erection of a new chapel, it can easily be con- verted into a school with large classrooms, a double roof having been arranged for this purpose. The length of the building is CB feet and the width about 27 feet, I while the total height is 44 feet. It is of brick faced with red sandstone, and relieved with Accrington bricks. The seating accommodation of the chapel is 300, and every endeavour has been matte to provide for convenience and comfort. At the morning service the Bishop, speaking of the new building, said some people would think the accommodation for 300 children as provided in the lower storey was far too large, but his experience was that such places wen° often found too sraall. When the project was first started the number of Catholics in the district was put at 400,but the num- ber now was 700. Of the total cost, there had oply been raised £950, leaving a debt, roughly speaking, of some £1,300 or £1,4-00, for which he made a special appeal,— The chief subscribers are the Rev. John Lennon, trustee of the Blackbrook and Orrell estate, £250; the Roman Catholic Bishop of Liverpool, £lOO, and altar cloths; missal, and stands ; the Ete,v. Arthur Powell (Buckley), £lOO, chalice, and altar cards ; Miss Coniston (Bolton- le-Sands), £2O ; and Miss Helen Mather (Hirchley), £5, and six candlesticks. The diocesan mission fund has contributed £250, and £63 was collected at the founda- tion-stone laying.

A UNIVERSAL BOON.

It is seldom that any discovery of science Is such as will benefit al: classes or pevie. Serpa of the greatest discoveries of the age, wnile proving beneficial to ttriusands, have also at feast for a time. injured other thousands. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

LOOTING THB NAVVIES BEEF.—PRECALITIONS AGAINST BURGLARY. Thomas Knockton was brought before the Warrington magistrates on Monday, on a charge of being a suspicious character, and with frequenting Pinner's Brow on Satur- day night with intent to commit a felony. P.C. Turner said that on Saturday night he was in company with P.C. Eitel when they heard cries of" police" and met prisoner with a parcel of beef under his arm, and a ship canal man following him saying that it was his. Pris- oner gave the beef to the man when he saw the officers, but the man did not wish to prosecute as he had no time to attend the court. They accordingly cautioned prisoner and told him to go home. At about 11.50 the same night, the officers again saw prisoner. He went into the doorway of shout half a dozen shops. As there had been so many burglaries of late, they arrested prisoner as a suspicious character. The Chief Constable said that in consequence of the number of burglaries, a tamer number of men than usual were on night duty,and were in plain clothes and they were instructed to appre- hend any man like prisoner, or woman either, who were about the streets at such an untimely hour. Prisoner was sent to gaol for one month. Too FOND OF LEATHER. Mary Ball was charged at Warrington Police Court on Monday, with stealing from a stall in the Market Place on Saturday night, two pairs of boots valued as. Id. the property of Joseph Frisby. Mr. Browne defended. Isaac Bowere. the manager of Frisby's shop, said that prisoner and another woman came to his shop. They were very talkative. and his suspicions were aroused. He watched her closely and saw her take a pair of boots and tried Vs put them into her pocket, but being unable to do so, she put them into her basket. lie then went for a policeman. and when he returned with P.C. Fish, the woman had another pair in her hands. She was taken into custody. Joseph Sixsmith, assistant, gave torroboratire evidence, and denied that prisoner had paid him 2s. 81. for the boots. Mr. Browne said that his case was that prisoner bought the first pair of boots, and was taking the second pair to show her husband who was a few yards away, and a witness, Elijah Page, swore to seeing prisoner pay 2s. &I. to an assistant at the stall. There was also undue violence used in arrest- ing prisoner, as her husband and son were strurk in the face, in trying to get an explanation of the matter. The magistrates considered the case proved, and fined pris- oner 10s. including costs or seven days' imprisonment. THE EXTENSIVE THEFTS HY YOUTHS. Jones (the chairman), at the close complimented Mr. Thndels, of Shrewsbury, for his prompt action in reporting the case to the police. DISPUTE WITH A LANDLORD. Benjamin Turton and Sarah Turton, 18. Lloyd-street, off Mill-lane, were each charged at the Warrington Borough Court on Friday, with assaulting and beating Jane Salt, 16, Thelwall-le,- oa the 29th ult. A cross- summons was also taken out against Jane Salt for assaulting Sarah Turton on the same date. Mr. Davies gppea red for Jane Salt, and Mr. Arthur Browne for the Turtons. Mr. Davies in opening the case said that his client formerly was the tenant of a shop belonging to

Mr. Turton in Lloyd-street, at a rent of 6s. 6d. per week. Mr. Turton had previously lived in the shop but had removed to the next door. Complainant paid Turton £l6 for stock on going in. On April last defendant said that he wanted the shop for his own purpose:, Miss Salt told him that he ought to give her time to find another place, but he made no reply. On May Ist defendant brought notice to quit in a week's time. She then asked him to take over tne stock but he refused to have anything. Witness had not gone by May 13, and a county court bailiff came to the shop and served a notice to quit. On May 15, complainant went to pay the rent but defendant refused to take it and on the 24th ult she received a county court summons for the rent. Com- plainant had by this time, a [shop in Thelwall-lane, and on the 29th ult she came with her mother, sister-in-law, and a man named Bedard, to pay her rent which amounted to 19s. 6d. Complainant and Beddard went into tho,house and complainant gave Turton the rent book and the money, and he only marked down 9s. 6d. After a little demur he altered it. Complainant then complained of defendant falsely representing the income of the shop. This seemed to enrage the defendants and Mrs. Turten gave her a blow on the head, and Mr. Turban also struck her several times and Beddard was pushed out of the house. At last she was rescued by her mother breaking in and pulling her out. Complain- ant. William Beddard, Mrs. Salt, and Mrs. Joseph Salt, corroborated Mr. Davies' statements. Mr. Browne denied the assault but said that complainant had called Turton some bad names, and they pushed her out. The magistrates considered the case against Mr. and Mrs. Turton fully proved, and ordered them each to be fined 10s. and costs or 14 days' imprisonment. They were also ordered to pay the advocaie's fee, the fine and costs amounting in all to £2 14s. The case against Jane Salt was dismissed.

WARRINGTON COUNTY CCURT.

THURSDAY. —More his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes. CLAIM FOR WAGES —Peter Buckley, a joiner, claimed El 13s. from William Rostock, builder, Stockton Heath, for work done.—Mr. Percy Davies, solicitor, appeared for the defendant. It appears that plaintiff was engaged by the defendant to affix a number of locks and latches to the doors of certain houses that had, been built. Plaintiff said he was on time-work and claimed Bd. an hour, which was what he called the" town wage."—Mr Percy Davies, asserted that plaintiff was engaged on piece work, and was only entitled to 7s. 4d. Defendant put in a counterclaim for 4s. which he alleged he had lent to plaintiff.—His Honour, after hearing the evidtmce was of opinion that plaintiff was employed on piecework and gave judgment tor defendant, both on the claim and on the counter-claim.

WARRINGTON V. LIVERPOOL —Quite 1,500 spectators witnessed the match at Arpley, on Saturday. The visitors took the first innings and were "all out" for 92 runs, Messrs. R. Smith and S. M. Crosfield, Sugg and J. J. Crosfield did the bowling. The latter gentleman took three wickets for no runs. Warrington started dis- astrously, for Mr. 3.3. Crosfield was bowled first over, both Sugg and H. Reynolds showed a partiality for Child's bowling, with tbe result that A. G. Steel took the ball from the junior professional. At 21 Reynolds was tempted out, whilst the next ball proved too much for S. M. Crosfield. Disasters did not stay here, for at n both Sugg and 0 Clarke were dismissed, the former being lbw, and the latter bowled next over. At 30 Tyler was c and b by A. G. Steel, and the prospects of War- rington gaining the day was indeed remote. However, on Toisey joining his captain some capital batting was witnessed, and the spectators were raised to a high pitch of enthusiasm, every run being hailed with cheers. At 47 Hampson bowled Voisey, and Clark came to the assistance of Smith. The latter now hit out, and bound- aries from both bowlers were chronicled. A change was deemed advisable at 78, Child resuming at his original end, and his first ball proved too much for Clark. Smith continued to bat confidently, but at 82 he was caught and bowled by Hampson, a decision which certainly did not meet with the approval of the excited spectators. His 34 runs were made up by fine cricket, displaying patience when necessary, With Podmore joining Bell, hopes were still entertained that Warrington would still gain the day, but at 88 the end came, Bell placing one ia the bands of short slip, the locals thus losing by four runs. Zattro to tin Obitor.

MINING ROYALTIES.

Sir,—l hope that anyone who has got it into his head that mining royalties have nothing whatever to do with trade will consider the facts given in the letter from " Observer," which you published last Saturday. These facts are interesting and thoroughly germane to the question. I rather regretted the last sentence in the letter, because I cannot agree with " Observer" that nationalisation of the land has yet risen above the horizon of practical politics. Some two or three years since a deputation waited upon Mr. Morley in Newcastle, and, as your readers may remember, consulted him among things on the question of nationalising railways. He reminded them of one diffi- culty, which they did not seem to have sufficiently con- sidered, namely, the trifling formality of payment. Aput from other questions, that is an obstacle in the way of land nationalisation, which, so far as I know,no responsible and influential politician of the present day is prepared to face. I have no intention, however, of entering upon a discussion of this question, nor is this passing reference to it my object in writing. May 1, therefore, in conclusion, again thank " Ob- server" for the thoroughness with which he is examin- ing the question of royalties, and jutt draw attention to the rather significant omission by your contemporary of one of his most striking illustrations, which has been carefully expunged from the letter, otherwise printed in full same as in "Examiner."—Yours, truly,

A. H. CgoSFIELD.

THE NAMING OF THE INFIRMARY WARDS. Sir.—l have no wish to dispute the claims of the per- sons of local worth whose names were attached on Thursday to the wards of the Infirmary ; but I do wish to express my astonishment that the name of the late Mr. Samuel Rigby was not chosen to receive that honour in connection with the Institution he did se much, both by gifts and labour, to es ablish it on its present basis. If lam not wrong, Mr. Rigby vras a prime mover in the erection of the builling which has now been extended, and I know that he was a generous donor and faithful worker in its interests. The inclusion of his name would have been no more than his due, and would, moreover, have been a proper recognition that the work and gene- rosity which support the Infirmary, are not alone be- stowed by Churchmen and Conservatives. As it is— quite erroneously and unjustly—it would seem as if only these parties had anything to do with this Institution. I can only charitably surmh.ethat the somewhat select and unrepresentative committee which has arranged these proceedings had forgotten that Mr. Rigby had been the 'zealous friend of the Infirmary before some of themselves had ever been much heard of in connection with it. But I certainly feel that I am not alone in the conviction that if any local name deserved perpetuation in this In- stitution it is the name of Rigby.—Yours faithfully,

A SUBSCRIBER TO THE EXTENSION. June 2nd, 1893.

Another correspondent writes: "Is the Infirmary given over to the Tories absolutely? I can understand a " Beamont Ward" and even a" Winmarleigh Ward," but why the others? Hai the name of "Samuel Rigby," passed out of the memories of Warringtonians entirely? cry shame on the Committee of Selection who could so cruelly ignore the work and interest which for many years wa% so conspicuously shown in this institution by one of the greatest local benefactors of our age. I fear that to be a Liberal and a Dissenter in this benighted town is in the eyes of the Tory, Church, Brewery party an unpardonable offence."
