OAbIBLING ON ADULT AND CHILDREN'S LIVES.

Sir,—No reply has yet appeared to my letter in your issue of May 20. Ido not feel surprised at the, fact after the repeated appeals for information in other quarters. The conclusion I have arrived at is,that these practises and methods must exist, or those who live by their existence would soon find thainselves out of wsrk. Now that I have stated a few of the grievances which I believe to exist, I will proceed to give a few specimen cases which can be proven by documentary and other evidence.

A working-man and his wife and five children were -entered in a collecting Friendly Society (having agencies in almost every town). Separate policies were issued on the adults,anuther on the two eldest children,and another on the three younger children. All this family were en- tered without a single signature being asked for or re- ceived by the agent. The two eldest children resided in another county, and were net seen by any official, as regards state of health, &c. No declaration was sub- mitted whether they were suffering from any disease or ailment, or even consumption. The agent only required Dames, ages, the same address for all, and, of course,the premiums and the bargain were struck,and policies same in two weeks. The man was astonished to see the poli- cies issued on such grounds. Ho appealed to agent, district manager, and chief secretary to enlighten him as to their genuinness, and from all came the same answer—that he need not alarm himself,because he was well and legally assured as well as the family, the chief secretary adding that hundreds of thousands of pounds had been received from and paid to members on such policies, and it was absurd to suppose anything wrong because they had received and paid such vast sums without interference. The man appealed to the "Legal Adviser" of a celebrated weekly paper, and the follow- ing is the public advice They are in the nature of gambling policies and are illegal by Statute, that is to say, the money professed to be assured by therm could not be recovered by law. Put such policies aro issued in enormous numbers,and apparently nothing but strong levislation will stop this gambling in lives. Presumably you have laid the facts before the society!' Having received such pronounced opinion upon the policies obtained without signatures, or a basis of contract, the lian applied for the return of premiums, or submit the enatter to the chief registrar, but the office was silent upon such a proposal,and the family must be lapsed and forfeit the premiums if he will not spend more money to test the conflicting evidence of legality.

As regards the legal aspect of such proceedings, and also upon the question whether an office can issue millions of policies, without signatures under £l2, and at same time demand signatures where the amount is £l3 and over ; r must leave such a matter for better informed men, but such a system opens out a clear gap to admit any number of unprincipled persons to secure money, they have no right to participate and share with honest assurers.

Other similar societies and companies use specified forms, with binding answers and declarations, and these have tm be signed by the assurer and witnessed. About 16 to 20 questions have to he answered, relating to age, health, disease, family history,if previously assured and policy nos. and to what office. This process applies also to children's proposals, and parents only have a right to enter a child, hut it such parents be dead, then the legal guardians of the child has the only right to enter a child. Where a false declaration is made by either assurer or agent there is ground for suspicion. These are two methods—which is the right one ? If carried out properly, which makes it difficult to do wrong ? lam not an opponent to life insurance but a supporter of it, and I shall do my utmost to defend it, when applied in a proper manner. To encourage a man te provioe for his wife and family against want and starvation when death shall summon him, is worthy of the commendation of eminent bishops, preachers, professors, and writers, who have spoken with no " uncertain sound." But who will point me to a single utterance, of any man, in praise of wholesale trading, by questionable methods upon other lives which I have OCCR SiOn to mention, viz., own life policies.—Yours faithfully,

JOHN MOORFIELD. 44, Parker-street, Warrington, May 31, 1893.

WITAT IS SALVATION? Sir.—ln your paper for weeks past there have appeared letters antagonistic to Christianity and the scriptures as we have them. First, we have Chrishna versus Christ; that Christina was original and antecedent ; that Christ copied his maxims and gave them to the world as his own ; that Christ was a plagiarist and an impostor, inspirating the works of another and better man. I for one, take such RtatementA r what they are worth, and say with the devil, " 1-14115 I know, and Paul I know," but who i Christna ? iNime hundred and ninety-nine thousand ott 01 every million of the world's population probably never hears of this ancient moral reference. What a pityl Is it too late now to bring him out ? By all means uhear la him, brirg his ti achines to light. The gospe! accordi,,g toChriAma would bean appropriate tit(, and would comn ad an extenSir''alo Then we have the Christian rah, on ani the bible

===== 0002601_18930603_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
accused of untold crimes,cruelty and murder, with wars innumerable. This I flatly deny. Christianity never injured a single individual. It is non-resistant. It is a doctrine of forgiveness, of love to all mankind, and enemies in particular, as declared in the Sermon on the Mount. Any student of the new testament must repudi- ate the statement that Christianity is an incitement to crime or wrong doing in any shape. I think that any- one bringing a charge against a system or a person should have their case founded in and built up in fact, but it is the same to-day as it was in Pilate's hall. They , could kill then, but the most that they can do in War- rington to-day is to indulge in contemptuous epithets and false charges. Chrhitian and fool have been with these wise people synonymous terms, ever since I can recollect. Fifty years ago and later, I was a reader of anti-Christian literature. I read Paine's " Age of Reason." He calls Paul a fool and Christ a bastard. I read Voltaire who was anxious "to crush the wretch." He predicted, ton, that the Christian religion would be extinct in 50 years, yet it has gone on increas- ing,and there are more Christians to-day than ever there were, and there will be more next year than now, if we take the past as a guide for the future. Among these anti-Christian writers you find much that is ribald un- scrupulous and scrurrilous. I have read some of the rav- ings of Ingersoll,and have listened to some of their crack lecturers, and what does it all amount to ? You ask ior bread and they give you a stone. You want knowledge and they say, believe nothing. Among the numerous anti-Christian authors I have read, I only came across one fair minded man. He wrote an octavo volume entitled " Materials for Thinking" in a gentlemanly, courteous, style. There were no contemptuous epithets nor scornful allusions, but fair argument and comment, such as I have never found in any other anti-Christian writer.

_ I contend, sir, that if I believe the bible as the scrip- tures of God, and in Jesus Christ as the Son of God and the Redeemer of the world, that no man or combination of men has any right to call me a fool for conscientiously believing what I am convinced is right. I further assert that man is a worshipping being, that there is within him an instinct to worship, find him where you may—whether barbariln or civilized, he feels there is a power superior to himself to which he is more or less amenable, a principle not inculcated by priests but implanted in his mind by some thing other than human influence. Uninstructed, he may err in his mode of worship and there is no true guide to worship but the scriptures, and what have the enemies of the gospel to charge the scriptures with as degrading to man or con- trary to science ? They inculcate purity of life in all its ramifications, chastity, honesty, justice as between man and man, faithful service by the servants,kind and liberal treatment by the master, care for the poor, reverence and love for the Supreme Being and an unselfli.l love to our fellow creatures of every creed ay.cl colour. Pure religion and undefiled before God and the Father in this, to visit the widows and fatherless in their affliction and to keep oneself unspotted from the world, and if religion sr anything called by the name is aught but this, it is impure and false, we have another simple formula. "Know,o man,what doth the Lord require of thee but to do justice, to love mercy, and to walk humbly with God." These are the formulas of a system that your cor- respondent raves so much about. Why do the heathen rage ? There is nothing so desperately wrong in these maxims as to cause such entire condemnation.

One of your correspondents is boiling over with excite- ment and rage against Hell and the Priests. He says Hell is not mentioned in the old Testament. I beg his pardon. If he looks he will find it occurs 29 or 30 times, and in the new Testament about a score of times. Jesus himself mentions the word some six or seven times with no uncertain sound. Why should there not be a Hell ! Shall guile, villainy and clever rascality stalk through this world, blighting earth's fairest treasures, robbing families and desecrating homes, unpunished ? For stich by all means let there be a Hell, and a hot one; for such who have m ide no restitution or shown no repentance, let them handed over to Him who has said, "venge- ance is mine, I will repay." Yet no one need go to Hell; there is a way of escape if we will accept it ; there is One who will deliver if we seek his aid. He will give us a goodly heritage among the children and in a plea- sant land, and we shall call Him our Father. Never mind the priests. There are none. There are no altars, and any one assuming the title of priest is in a false posi- tion and misleads those who believe him. We can go to God without a professional priest. We have a great High Priest, Jesus the Son of God, who is gone into the heavens. Let us, therefore, come boldly to a throne of grace, and find mercy and grace to help in time of need. —I am, yours respectfully, Sankey Bridges.

CYMRO.

Sir,—l should like throne' the medium of youn valu- able paper to answer" Snodgrass," " Clifford Dix," and " lona," on the questions of Immortality and Salvation. But a word before I start concerning the clergymen of the present day. The 4th verse of the IV. of Hosia, des- cribes them well : " Thou haat rejected knowledge," is their work ; " My people are destsoyed for lack of knowledge," is the result of that work, " And I will re- ject thee that thou shalt he no priest to me," is the pun- ishment of that work. No man should dare to take upon himself the work of Christ, unless he has been bidden of Him, unless he has heard that same voice which once sounded on the slopes of Olivet and in the streets of Jerusalem, saying" Follow me." And there must be no mistake about it. It must be genuine, as St. Paul's call was genuine,as Baxter's call was genuine,as Wesley's call was genuine. There are mere men devil-called than Christ-called ; for one called of Christ there are a thou- sand called of Satan. When you see a man "moping and mowing," and drawling the service out until he has but ten minutes for the sermon, you may know that that man is doing the devil's works. When you see a man drunk with the spirit of God—uncovering with irresistible speech the limitless wealth of the Bible, un- covering it as none can but one whose lips have been touched with a live coal from off God's altar, you may safely say that that man is a Christ-made fisher of men. Christ has so called me, and shortly I shall come forth endued with His power to preach the Living Word. At presant I am very busy with my preparation and have not time but very slightly to touch upon the two ques- tions. For the rpst they must wait until my hour is come.

Now concerning the Immortality of Man. The heathen philosophers of Greece and Rome demonstrated with their mighty intellects the immortality of the soul, but they never were so illogical as to try and demonstrate the immortality of the body,when they had evidences every day to the contrary. Let me take you to the garden of Eden and there show you a Being with burning glory surrounding Him, bending over another,inert, dead, His exact image without the glory. See Him put His mouth to its nostrils and breathe into them. Watch that Eternal Breath quicken that body, causing the blood to flow, the brain to work, the eyes to open, the limbs to move. Watch it stand upon its feet a perfect god ! Start not my friends when I tell you ye are Gods, in that ye have some of the breath of Gcd within you—called the soul—and so are higher than angels, and are des- tined far a glorious work, if ye fulfil the end for which ye were born. In what beast did God breathe the breath of Christ ? A living soul is but an Hebraism ap- plied to anything that breathes. Now that breath which Adam had within him would have caused his body to have lived for ever if he had never sinned. And he and his children would have gone on from glory unto glory, until they had reached that perfect state which now we can not reach except through the crave, and the atoning blood of Christ. Sin, and what an awful thing sin must be, when it is an impos- sibility on the part of the Eternal Father to forgive it, , that He had to visit the sins of all who trust la Christ upon Christ, from Adam down to the latest child of time, for Adam and Eve had a lively faith in the pro- mise of One who should be a propitiation for their sins and the sins of their future children. Sin, I say,caused the vitality of the soul to decrease gradually, until from an average ate of 900 years, the average age is, what now ? And the body at the last day—wherever its parts be scattered, though it has been eaten by cannibals, or burned and the ashes cast into every river on the face cf the earth—God shall gather it together from the aireand sea, and land, and shall create it anew, and it shall be the same yet not the same. "He that ;lath ears to hear let him hear.' "Though after my skin worms destroy this body yet in my flesh shall I see God," writes one who was a contemporary of Abraham. Just a word on the power of God. There are no such things as miracles. The word miracle has been in- serted in the Bible to suit our present understandings. A miracle is nothing but the knowledge of laws about which neither you nor any angel in heaven know any- thing—though one man does profess to tell us that man emanated from a fish! and another from a monkey ! How Satan can blind a mall and puff him up in his fleshly mind ! "The natural man receiveth not the things of the Spirit of God; for they are foolishness unto him; neither can he know them, because they are spiritually discerned." I. Cor. 2. 14. How they laugh at it. Yet how worthy it is of the Being whose know- ledge is infinite, and to that knowledge we shall come in the eternal hereafter, when we are invested with the 'heirship of God and the joint heirship of Christ—when we shall be equal to Christ in purity, and knowledge, and power—if so be that we suffer with Him.

Now concerning hell and salvation. Hell simply means the covered place, and comes from an old Saxon verb meaning to cover. It has beets translated, unfor- tunately, by the words "pit," "deep," and "grave" in the Old Testament. But we must distinguish between such sentences as "And they took him and cast him into a pit" Genesis xxxvii. 24; and "They went dawn alive into the pit" Num. xvi. 33. Jacob says: "I will go down into the grave unto my son mourning" Genesis xxxvii. 35. Now, he uses the same Hebrew word (Sheol, which is equal to the Greek Hades) which is used concerning Korah, Dathan, and Abiram, and we are very sure he did not mean the Sheol of the lost, for the people of God had a very lively faith in the hell of the damnedois anyone who reads the O.T. with any degree of carefulness will soon see. The places where men go after death are places of waiting for the final judgment—for the blood of Thrist gains you an entrance into heaven but your works shall gain you higher and lower places in the future heavens, and the same thing applies t hell, Your despising the Atonement sends you to hell, but your deeds of wickedness gains you higher and lower places in the future hells. At present it is a state of waiting and both places go under the title

===== 0002601_18930603_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JUNE 3, 1893. NOTP:S ON GAMES AND 1 CIVILIANS IN CAMP. AMILETICS.

of Hades, or hell, or simply the covered or hidden places, but the bad are divided from the good by a great gulf, which is the irrevocable decree of God. But they are not purgatory—as the tree falls so shall it lie—as death leaves you,so shall judgment find you. This is your only state of probation; if you cannot believe the evidences of Christianity here, which all true men from Adam downwards have believed, you shall have no chance of embracing their truths hereafter. Dives knew with a terrible knowledge that there was no hope for him, though he now saw his terrible mistake. And the flames which wrappeo Dives round have nothing to do with your fire. Do you suppose that that could harm a spirit, either now or when it takes again its body? No! No! No! It could not singe a hair. 'The words fire and brimstone have been placed to give you a faint idea of the terrible punishment of a being eaten up with the awful fever of the knowledge that he has wilfully thrown away heaven and Christ, and trampled them under his feet, and that God did everything to bring him to Christ, but he would not come, and God could not force him— and that now he is naked under the eye of Him who is a consumin, fire! lieb. xii. 23. Something far worse than your fire my friends. Christ died to save you from that. His death on the cross was only the means. Dur- ing those 38 hours he suffered the whole punishm,mt of all who have trusted in Him and all who shall trust in Him—the whole punishment of the fierceruss of the wrath of Almighty God, concentrated into 38 hours! What wonder that He—the bravest man the world has ever known—what wonder, I say, that He should cry out aloud in awful terror, sweating drops of blood, and that God in pity should send an angel to whisper of His high ernprise and the glorious joy attending self-sacrifice —and that high emprise, my friends, is what is called salvation.

There are more things in heaven and earth than are dreamt of in your philosophy.—Yours truly, SLAVE OF GOD.

" The better part of valour is—discretion."

Sir,—Having asked the question "What is salvation ?" without any adequate 'answer from those entitled if not in duty bound, to furnish one, I now offer to your readers the view I take as to what salvation is. Not be- ing content with the ordinary hand-me-down religion of the day, I have sought for a reason for this cry about Salvation. I find it to be another case of great cry and little wool.' The " reasons " are in my opinion anything but sound and satisfactory. Hence I offer some reasons which I think entirely destructive of the theory- of dog- matic salvation. As to the value of these reasons I shall be happy to hear the opinion of any of the champions of inherited orthodoxy. Salvation then is the essence of Christianity. Without salvation the popular religion would be as Hamlet without the Prince ; as with, it is a Midsummer Nights Dream, or Much Ado About Nothing. I know there are some who wear, for ornamental pur- poses an eviscerated christianity, minus this and other elements of the faith. But these notwithstanding, sal- vation, is the raison d'etre of biblical Christianity ; that is, redemption from the effect of an original sin com- mitted by a specially created pair. Now here your readers have placed before them the elements of plain but vital issues. Here we shall see that the theory of salvation depends upon the accuracy of the theory of special creation and the occurrence of a specially-ereated first human pair. Now modern science has disposed very effectually of these traditional fables. Here we find ourselves at the junction of two divergent paths—the way of verifiable facts, and that of unverifi- able fable. On the one hand we have a traditional fable whist' can be traced to a semi-barbarous pre-Babylonian people ; the other the conclusions of science drawn from facts and observations quite destructive of these myths. Hence I see only one course open to a reasoning being ; leaving the other course open to those who can compromise the truth and assimilate incongru- ous ideas. The cultured preacher of to-day who teaches this fantastic doctrine is, by implication, denying the best results of the best science of the day. Such a man can only be either a muddle-headed incongruist, or a deliberate deceiver of the people. The teaching of such a man is calculated to do injury to the young. But just as reasonably and successfully might these Gamaliels teach their young disciples that 3 by 3 are 12 as that man is a fallen specific creation. The science of to-day says that 3 by 3 are nine, and that man has risen rather than fallen, and been evolved rather than specially created. How long will the clerics maintain that 3 by 3 are 12?

And now a few words as to such of your correspon- dents who have touched this question. The only avowed believer in this dogma who had the courage of his con- viction was " An Old Chartist," and he furnished us with reason for his belief which was not strange when treat- ing of an unreasonable dogma. As to "Clifford Dix" and " Snodgrass "I may say that my object was not so much to get the opinion of those who see the matter from my point of view as to ascertain what those who uphold the dogma had to say of it when viewed by the search light of modern science. From this they have wisely abstained. The dogma is only to be maintained by leaving about it the dust and cobwebs of ecclesiastic tradition. Ignorance is its only justification. As to " Psychic," who writes in last issue I would recommend to him a course of reconsideration. His reasons for ad- vocating or deprecating the discussion of religious sub• jects in newspapers are curious. If the discussion seems to countenance his preconceived views, the discussion is right. If, on the other hand, the result be adverse to these foregone conclusions, then he "swivels round " and lifts his hands in holy horror because it does not " further the cause of christianity." He seems more concerned about that than the furtherance of the cause of truth.

On the subject of Salvation ecclesiastic history is only repeating itself. Science has before now swept away the dogma of a geocentric system—the llatns of the earth and the truly parochial idea that the sun,nr. oon and stars were subordinated to it as light givers. As these doctrinal absurdities have been relegated to the limbo of worn out beliefs which none now care to defend—so will special creations and its attendant myth salvation Find a place in the lumber room of the rej...icted creeds. Men are coming to see that no honour can accrue to the Supreme by the ascription to Him of erroneous revela- tions.—Yours truly, 'SOS.

" Snodgrass " writes: "Your correspondent "Psy- chic," says I "blankly deny" roan's tutor° existence, and talks of "swivelling round" in consequence. I have never denied man's future sxistenc3—only the bible account of It; neither have I denied the existence of a God, but only the bible account of Him. There are great distinctions here. Every assertion, biblical or profane, niust be subjected to criticism, and for ciitic- ism to be effective and useful, the public press must lend its valuable aid. Why then should orthodoxy be allowed full scope, while other opinions are only allowed "reasonable bounds," and "to a certain extent" P Would you deny us that liberty which you allow your- self? Can we damage Truth? I beg " Psychic " to look at this question in a purely uubiass ed way."

" Anisos," referring to " Isos," says " In one of his letters, he writes with all the dignity of an oracle "This appears to be an age of dissillusion, an age of more or less awakening to the unrealibity of lately cherished be- liefs. Now, such an avrakesing is not always pleasant." Undoubtedly so, but the unfortunate thing is that " Isos " has substituted for them nothing positive. For such truth, he exhibits a special dislike, if not nausea. What seems so extraordinary is that he evidently re- gards it as a great acquisition, something indeed of which to boast. This, however, is an utter mistake. A physical constitution which rejects every kind of food is diseased and Is far from being an enviabl. one. It is the same with the mental constitution which rej.cts every positive truth, co matter whether designated agnosticism or advanced thought, such a condition is an indication of disease and should be avoided like poison."

[With Hip letters of this week. the correspondence on this subject is closed.—ED.

CRICIRT.

There was one feature -in the- -Match bstween Warring- ton and Liverpool on Saturday, wbieh cast a shadow over an otherwise enjoyable game. This was when Smith was " caught " out by Hampson. At least Mr. flampson and the Umpire said Ms was caught, but Mr. Smith vehemently protested that he was not caught, and the majority of the spectators were of the samo opinion. From what I saw of the matter in dispute I I think it would only have been fair to have given Smith , the benefit of the doubt. However, as it was, Smith again came out with the highest score, he having m Lde 34 runs.

Although the visitors brought with them a team of high repute, they were only able to slip past the tape— to use a racy term—by four runs more than the home team. The question is, where would they have been had Smith been given the benefit of that doubt. A. G. Steel, Livernool's champion, did not fill the spectators with any considerable amount of wonder, or admira- tion, although he played a fair game. The "gate" were expectant of good things from him. This was perhaps because his reputation had preceded him ; and they didn't get tne anticipated things perhaps because he hadn't overtaken his reputation. He managed to take four wickets for 42 runs and made 18 runs himself. Mr. J. J. Crosfield, as a contrast managed to take three , wickets for no runs. Not much difference, is there?

The bowling was led off My Mr. S. M. Crosfield and Mr. Smith. and they were aided ,by some fielding that has not seen its like in the neighbourhood for a consider- able period. In fact the excellent fielding of cur eleven is becoming quite a noticeable feature in their play this season. Although cot taking many wickets for 131 runs that were scored against him Smith exhibited some bowling feats that were worth witnessing. The fact of his taking A. G. Steel and the Rev. E. B. Brutton for 25 runs was encouraging enough, but it was the surpris- ing manner in which he charged the nature of every other ball, that interested everyone. It was what is des- cribed by a colloqualism as " tricky." That it was a tough contest can be seen from the fact that the home scored 88 against 92.

The second team did comsideraloly better in their match with Bolton second team. They clinked the Willow to the tune of 121 against 111. Bibby took three wickets for 14 runs for Warrington. With the bit Jefferson was positively brilliant, and he was only run out when he had scored 42 runs. There had been a con- siderable amount of scoffing going on, I am told, but at he end of the game Scoffin became very much in evi- dence, when he ran up 15 and was not out.

The Sankey Wesleyans had very little difficulty in giving St. Helens Y.M.C.A.,a severe thrashing on Satur- day. The match began, to compare small things with great, in a somewhat similar manner to the sensational match between Lancashire and the Australians, as on the Y.M.C.A. going in first, they lost two wickets for no runs, and had the Wesleyans paid a little mere attention to the fielding, the visitors would not even have scored 31. Watkina proved very destructive with the ball taking seven wickets. The Wesleyans passed their opponents score with the loss of only one wicket and finally ran up a score of 75. Bailey and Clare punished the bowlers and the former had scored 28 before he was dismissed by a fine catch. Clare had got well set when he was run out through a misunderstanding with his partner after scoring 22. The Wesleyans had some . strong opponents to meet during the next four weeks, as they meet Briek-st., and Friars Green to-day, and Pen- keth Friends, Widnes White Bose and Earlestown Wes- leyans very shortly. If they come through these unscathed, they may have a good shake hands with themselves.

The Juniors of the same ilk are keeping up their record as boy champions. They " took on" Bewsey-road Wes- leyans and didn't let them off until they had scored 73 runs a:ainst the Bewsey-road players' 20. These young lions will soon have to seek higher game; hut I wouldn't advise them to challenge the Warrington Ist eleven—at least, not just yet,

• Perhaps the Juniors would like to take on the Working- men's Mission Club again. I see they met Buckley-st. on Saturday, and ran up the grand total of 19 against 105! They ought to be easy game.

AURTON CYCLE & MACHINE CO., LTD.

It. will be seen from our advertising columns that the Burton Cycle Co., of Warrington, is being con- verted into a Limited Company, with £lO,OOO capital. The concern is well established, and the premises are admirably adapted for the 'cycle trade, Considering the genius of Mr. Burton, and his untiring energy, coupled with the fact that many famous riders have pronounced his system of gearing and anti-vibra- tors ahead of everything, we think investors would find the new Company a good speculation. We learn that , the Company recently supplied to the order of the Pope Manufacturing Co.—one of the wealthiest firms in the world—one of their chainless machines,which latter have la good reputation in America. The accountant certifies that fitto August 29, 1891, to May 2s, 1892, being nine , mond/Where was a profit of over 25 per cent., on the 'capital employed. The Company acquires " The Bur- ton, Cycle Co., of the Knutsford-read works, Warrington, together with the very valuable English, Colonial, Con- tinental and all foreign, except American, patent rights of Mr. W. C. Burton, and the vendor agrees to accept as purchase money for all the plant, tools, stock in trade,gigs,templates, patterns,goodwill,&c ,of the Burton Cycle Co., and all his before-mentioned patent rights, arid further improvements during the term of his engage- ment,the sum of £1,590 in cash and £3,500 in fully pd' up shares in the company. The Vendor undertakes that upon £3,500 shares to be alloted to him no dividend shall be payable to him until six per cent, per annum has been paid upon all the other shares. The vendor not to receive dividend on amount unpaid on shares, but to Lave five per cent. on the fully paid up portion. Mr. Burton, the vendor, is a practical engineer and cycle manufacturer, is well known and successful in his business, and as he will continue manager of the com- pany for five years at a moderate salsrv, we predict a anod future for the Company and large dividends to tl,e investors.

CYCLING.

I have received a letter from a " Lady cyclist," in which she applauds the suggestion that has been made in this column that a ladies' cycling club should ho or- ganised. At present, however, she thinks that her com- panions are too shy to organise a club,as they are looked upon with disapproval and termed masculine by their non-riding companions.

Liverpool was honoured by a visit from the Warring- ton C.C. (01 Saturday. Thirty members turned out including those two Restraining Individuals,the Chaplain and the Secretary. To say that ,a jolly time was not spent would be untrue, for who remembered this club of "congenial souls" not extracting pleasure from any run—even though it happened to be a "run in" for any member, which however, I hasten to say has never happened.

The Wheelers also visited Liverpool, but they again failed to muster more than a dozen ; which is a pity. After the inner man had been regaled at Liverpool, New Brighton was visited, and tho return journey was com- menced at about the time of the gloaming.

SwlmmiNa.

Last week I mentioned the fact that a swimming club was being organised in connection with the new baths. Nothing definite has yet been accomplished, but it is expected that within a fortnight the organisation will he an accomplished fact, or to indulge in a "goak," it will be going on "swimmingly."

Whilst on this subject, perhaps it would not be unin- teresting to mention that so long ago as 1879 there was a capital swimming club in existence in the town, the members of which have now chiefly overgrown any pro- pensity for indulging in aquatic exercise, at least of a very active character, Mr. Harry Toll, tobacconist, of Sankey-st., was the first captain,Mr. J. Lyon Whittle, the present Town Clerk, was the hon. sec., and Mr. E. England was the treasurer. Councillor Josiah Whittle was made presidenb, and there was a committee of the following :—Messrs. M. Smart, S. Kenyon,James Sutton, W. Skelton, R. Wood. E. Caldwell, T. Welsby, Charles Halton, N. Povrlson, and J. Beswick.

This club became very strong, and successful sports and feleg were common things with it. Hut at length its dissolution came ; and the Corporation of Warrington were the cause of it. Seeing that the clubs' funds were augmentineconsiderably, the than civic fathers thought to obtain some of the "spoil," and acctrdingly put up the price of the season tickets from 153. to one guinea. There was a dispute, the result of which was that the club broke up. This, I hope, will convey its moral to the present Corporation.

There have been four times as many season tickets sold for the baths during the past two weeks, than were sold in any four months last year. This looks well !

MrscELLANEous.

The much talked of race between J. Green, and James Walsh of Warrington, came off at St. Helens, on Satur- day, at the Star Inn Grounds. Eighty yards was the distance run, and althongh heavy odds were laid on Walsh, Green came in easily half a yard ahead, amid much excitement. Green was trained by Andrew Hol- land, nf Wigun. and Wakh by Sunderland. of Radcliffe. Green is coming out very smarty there is no doubt, and some people think a run between Worrall and him would be interesting.

I notice that at the Everton Football Club Sports, hell on Saturday, on the Goodison Park ground, three Of our local athletes acquitted themselves in creditable style. Dainteth captured second place in the mile bicycle race. There was 7,000 spectators at the meeting, which was excellently managed.

ANNIVECSARY SERMONS.—On Sunday, the annual ser- mons were preachad in the Wesleyan Chapel, morning and evening. The preacher was the Rev. J. Grange Bennett, of Didsbury College. The attendance in the morning was moderate. Special hymns were sung at the morning and evening services by the scholars and choir, who were arranged on a gallery by the organ. At the morning service the minister gave a short address to the little ones in a very pleasing way. For his text he took Judges 3 chap. part of 15 verse. The minister drew a very vivid picture ef the deliverance of the ! people of Israel by Ehud and held him up as a pattern for the zealcus Christian. In the afternoon a sacred Oratoria entitled " Christ and his Soldiers " was ren- dered by the scholars and teachers. The piece consisted of solos and chorus parts which were exceptionally well rendered. The following ladies and gentlemen sang the solos. Sopranos : Misses A. Hughes, A. Boardman, E. Watson, L. Bowles. Contraltos : Miss Hilda Garnett and Miss A. Moses, Tenors : ' Messrs. It, E. Garnett. J. T. lindden, A. Sid. Bass :D. Ellison and J. A. Smith. Mr. W. J. In the evening a splendid sermon was preached from Daniel 3 chap., 18, 17, and 18th verses. • e collections amounted to £22 17s. 4d. which is an increase of 7s. on last year's collections. Much praise is due to Mr. James Watson to whom the musical part of the services was entrusted.

IWUGHING IT AT CONWAY.

By LUKE BLUNT.

One square meal before we set out from Llan- dudno to Conway camp, was what we wanted. So we set about finding a suitable restaurant. This is always a diffiult task : and it is always not crowned with glorious and encouraging success. We marched all down the main street, and stopped opposite at least 50 cook shops, and closely and carefully perused the bill of fare in each window. Notwithstanding the fact that they all detailed the same tempting items, we were not satisfied, and had the main street of Llandudno contained an- other 100 such establishments we should have in- spected each one. Necessity at length compelled us to make a selection; it was the only restaurant in a bye street which we had casually caught sight of, and we entered without even consulting the bill of fare. This shows how perversely and errati- cally is constituted human inclination. In a quarter of an hour, after sitting down, the debate upon the character of the viands we should have had ended. Simpkins had lost his proposition for devilled sole by two oat of three" tosses," and as Franklin was neutral my advocacy for Welsh-rabbit succeeded. We rang the bell, a waitress came ; we gave the order, and she went. In about a quarter of an hour she came back and said " Well an Inglish ra-beet doe ? " Nothing but one of those much talked of Welsh bunnies would do for us ; and we said so,and left. We hunted up six more restaurants, but they hadn't any Welsh rabbits : completely sold out they said. In the meantime Iliad dilated so eloquently upon the delicious flavour of the animal we required, that all were in a frenzy for a taste. At the eighth establishment our flagging courage was sustained, for the waitress showed no concern at the order but rushed in a minute later all hustle, and clat- tered down three plates and a cruet-stand. This elated us, and before she had been gone a minute, we fancied we could detect the appetising odour of the dish. We waited—five, eight, ten minutes— and the rabbit hadn't arrived. We called the wait- ress, and she assured Us with all the English words she could remember, that it wouldn't be long. We read all the current literature lying about the tables; then inquired again and they said "Just a minute—no more what-efer." Prolonged disappointment had made us moody and cast the dull, depressing cloak of the" blues" about us. At length happiness returned with one bound : two waitresses were rushing to us with plates. "At last," we muttered, and clutched our knives. But oh ! had some one taken us with a snap-shot camera, what a treasure would have been the por- trait, what a picture of despair and rage. Each of those plates simply contained a thick square toast sandwich with a thick layer of toasted cheese between ; and they had the boundless, undiluted, impertinence to say it was Welsh rabbit ! The bread was square and about an inch thick and the presence of the cheese was almost imperceptible. This is something like a Side view of it.

_

A WELSH RABBIT.

Rage wouldn't let us say mucli to that gigantic colossal fraud of a landlord. We simply asked hint if he thought we came from Lymm ! It was the squarest meal we ever had—or want.

Away from there we went, and caught the first train to Deganwy where we intended to cross the ferry to the camp on the Marsh. It was eventide. and we saw a glorious picture as we crossed the river—everything was tinted with the ruby hues of the setting sun. As we neared the opposite side of the ferry, two figures draped in white could be e9en standing motionless near the boathouse ; one was evidently a woman in her night gown. We hastily pointed this out to the boatman but he could only grin and say " Maria of Leeverpool !" We got him to stop a bit to see if Maria at least would take the hint and move. But with appalling composure she held her ground I This was dis- heartening. Simpkins draped his face with a Cambric handkerchief and as we neared the bank, Franklin and I prepared to rush past the unblush- ing creature with our backs towards her, and we had commenced to do it, when a loud laugh from Simpkins made us face about. He was examining the figures. One was a woman swarthed in a virgin white dress that hadn't any hustle. She was a large full-sized figure—head from a ship named "Maria of Liverpool," which had been wrecked on the beach, so we were told. The other figure was that of a man. He also was the figure head of a wrecked ship. His attire was that of the last century, and his posture was not, it seemed to me, dignified enough to have been designed for the vessel in the service of the Chaplain of the Fleet. Re had a one aidei grin on his face—as though he'd lost consider- able teeth—and he had an appearance of jumping forward, as though from the intrusion of a pin in his rear. Why those figures are left there to inflict temporary shocks upon people we couldn't find out; but we were told we were not the first victims. One old short-sighted dame had gone so far as to commence an indignant sermon at Maria, upon modesty, and virtue.

Presently we reached the myriad ef snowy white tents and having sought out tho Warrington battalion we were taken to the little caavas house that was to be our residence. Camping out seems to be a fascinating, Bohemian mode of existence when viewed from that unmarred contemplation born of inexperience. So we found out by and by. It is two miles from the camp to Conway town ; so we did this walk after depositing out baggage in the tent. When we commenced our return journey, it was quite dark, and a heavy drenching shower had set in. We reached camp; everything was quiet and still, and dark, for the bugle had sounded for lights out. We passed the guard all right and then commenced a hunt for our tent. We didn't seem to be able to locate it any way ; they all looked the same. We adjourned to an elevated mound near the water supply tap at one end of the camp and took a survey. Simpkins said he had his eye on our tent now, s ire. if it would not move. So he lead the way and we followed. In a bit he fell over some cordage supporting one of the tents. We did the same. When the awakened and enangered inmate had quieted down again we quietly stole off after Simpkins once more. Presently he stopped and commenced to grope an entrance into a tent. But a low silvery voice, just a little hoarse by the cold said : " Yah ! you - thieves, would yer ? ' and then a boot whizzed amongst our faces. So we left there ; and took another survey. Our guide said he knew he'd got it this lift, and we followed again. In a bit we reached our tent, as we thought when we heard this conversation going on inside : "Nap." A clinking of money. "I towd thee to put that Jack on ; but clever bike, you wouldn't.

" Well, what if I didn't put it on." " Oh ; nnthina extra much." " Well _then, .het thee yed."

We had not ipvited congenial company of this kind to our tent, so we concluded we were wrong again, and left. More hunting followed and eventu- ally we were arrested by the guard as suspicious characters. Explanations, however, led to our delivery from the bewildered state we had fallen into, for the guard conducted us to our tent. But we didn't seem to enjoy that bewitching sensation that books speak of when theircharacters ere doing the " camping" trick. We felt tolerably miserable. We had lit a short candle and stuck it on a drum head and gazed around. Our clothes were dripping with rain; the rain was trickling through the canvas,the trusses of hay upon which we had to sleep looked hard and uninviting, and every gust of wind crept under the bottom of the canvas and blew and paper and litter about the sandy floor, and crept up our legs. Having disrobed ourselves of the wet clothing we prepared to roll ourselves up in wet blankets, when there was a rustling sound in the convas. We looked and saw a helmet gradu- ally emerge under the tent ; then a face came. trhe face had eyes that looked startled, and it said " Shi ! Lemma in won't yer." Then without wait- ing for an invitation the remainder of the volun- teer about " three in the wind " crept into the tent, When he got in he poked Franklin in the ribs and said " Well-hic-lad and-hows-hic-th' owd hen." After a stupid silence, he commenced to chuckle and sing: " They're-hic after me hic after me, To cash me-hic ish everyone's deshire, -

Then from a fragmentary explanation which he gave, we found that he had been chaQed by the guard, and to prevent, arre.it had sought the pro- tection of our tent. We were in hopes that he would go to sleep and let us slumber. But we were diAoppointed. Ile dropped into a maudlin crying fit, and said he always got abused when he "come ew't without are Sall." He said he wouldn't come to the camp or anywhere ele again with-

out her, lie kept this up for about an hour and then wearied and irritated, Franklin rose and said " Old chap,show us the canteen,will you," and they went out together ; and when Franklin returned he said that as his companion had fallen over that cordage, evidently from exhaustion, he had, out of compassion, left him there to rest his weary body —and catch rheumatism.

Maybe we would have slept the remainder of the night had not a kind friend dropped in and warned us to keep a stick handy for the rata. But as it was, we never closed our eyes and rose the next morning more fatigued than we were the previous night. At the sound of the five o'clock reveille we were glad enough to get moving about to restore the circulation of the blood. But we now discov- ered that Simpkins, who had not worn an overcoat last night, was unable to put on his trousers they were so wet. How was he to get to the mess for breakfast P was the question that now presented itself. At length we hit upon a lucky idea. He was to go to breakfast without his trousers, And he dia it. Ile wore my long overcoat which reached down to his ankles—and not a soul save ourselves knew he had not his nether garments on. He had to remain in the tent the whole of the morning, whilst we rambled about the tents on the Marsh Things went smoothly enough during the day' except that we all developed prodigious colds. There were no fires in camp,so one had no chance of getting artificial heat. This night turned out almost as bad as the previous one. When we had got used to the wind's threats to blow the tent down, and tried to settle ourselves down to sleep, it was a failure, because about every five minutes we had to evoke each others aid to get extricated fiorn the holes between trusses of hay, which we had not noticed. Tne next day was wet; everything was wet—the tent was, the ground was, the beds were, our clothes ' were. We passed another night of sleeplessness, chiefly in hunting imaginary rats, which turned out to be pieces of rustling paper. The third day we rose again; our clothes and boots were warped and full of irritating sand, our faces were stubbly, our hair was unkompt,tor we had neither mirror or comb. We felt dirty and miserable, but told all in- quirers we were in a field of clover,so to speak. Early in the afternoon Simpkins said that one of those in- explicable impressions of foreboding about his wife's safety was troubling him ; he didn't like to ignore such warnings of providence, so he was going to mov he said. Slim tly before tea, Franklin had a severe attack of rheumatism in his leg,so we decided to go at once, and we travelled those two miles to the station in 20 minutes, Franklin, brave fellow ! shooting along as though he really hadn't got rheu- matism. This is about all I remember of the camp —except that when Simpkins got home he told h is wife he was surprised she had not been anxious about him, and she replied that she had been com- forted from tribulation by the fact that" a special providence watched over drunken men, boys and idiots."

THE NEW WARRINGTON PUBLIC HALL AND OFFICES.

SUGGESTED SCHEME.

--- The following report presented by the Town Hall, iSzc. Committee, has been approved by the General Purposes Com tnittee,subject to such altera- tions as may be found necessary, and it will be laid before the Town Council on Tuesday evening next. " The Town Hall, Park, and Baths Committee, having conisdered the resolution of the Water Committee, as to the provision of office accommodation, beg to bring be- fore the notice of the General Purposes Committee the following recommendations on the subject ;—That it is desirable to form a comprehensive scheme for the whole requirements. The scheme that appears best to lend itself to the circumstances is that the work should he proceeded with in three sections. Ist—That a complete suite of all the Municipal Officers required, and of which a list has been turnißliod by the Borough Surveyor, should be built in a block on the west side of the Town Hall, forming a wing al it were, to the present building,and that this block should also include an assembly room above. 2nd.—To extend the present Council chamber (prob- ably by lengthening into the garden), and to provide such rooms as may seem to be further required, but not materially to interfere with the general structure, but to leave the entrance hall intact ; the Borough Surveyor's and Town Clerk's offices being in their present position.

. - 3rd.—To include plans for new police department in an eastern wing, and to consist of court room for borough sessions, Chief Constable's office, clerk's office, men's day and parade room, cells, &c., &c., and also a county sessions room and a county court room, (for which two latter no doubt a reasonable rent would be paid to the borough fund). It will thus be seen that by building or altering in three sections the firstand most necessitous requirements could be proceeded with, leaving over the other two until such time as it might be deemed expendient to proceed with them also. In accordance with this scheme we should propose to issue an advertisement, inviting architects to send in competitive drawings and plans for the whole of the works, offering, a premium of £lOO for the best and selected design and of £5O fer the second best, and that such plans should become the property of the Corporation. That the architect whose plans may be selected will not necessarily be employed to carry out any works, except such as may be proceeded with dur- ing the next three years, which will probably embrace the first or western wing. 'I ha% a duly qualified archi- tect shall be appointed as assessor of the several plans, and shall report in detail of those selected for the first and second premium."

ALLEGED BREACH OF CONTRACT.

ACTION BY A FRODSLIAM CORN MERCHANT.

At the Warrington County Court, on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Ffoulkes and a jury, James Edwards, corn merchant, Frodsham, sued William Turner Brereton, a farmer, of Preston-on- for .E5O, made up of £4O paid on account for some oats, and £lO for damages alleged to have been sustained. Mr. Mulholland, of Liverpool, in- structed by Mr. Burton, of Runcorn, appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. 0, Swift, of St. Helens, in- structed by Mr. Robert Davies, for the defendant.— Mr. Mulholland said that his client's businesa con- sisted in buying corn from farmers upon the best terms. He and defendant had spoken about some oats which were in the stack at the beginning of January, but nothing came of the matter, but later, Mr. arereton gave plaintiff a small sample of the oats. In Fobruary Edwards went to the farm at Preston-on-'th-11111, and defendant's wife took him into the granary to look at oats which were now thrashad. Ho took a sample with him. On March 22nd, Edwards and Breretln met each °thiir at the Little Horse Shoe Inn, Warrington, and Brereton started the subject of the oats and asked Edwards how much he would give for them. It was finally settled that the plaintiff should pay 2s. Bd. per bushell for white nats,and 2s.2d.for blackoats,and the bar- gain was concluded,in which it was decided that plaintiff should find the bags, and defendant fill them and take them to Preston Brook Station. Defendant wished to be allowed a little time to send the oats as he was very busy with his horses at the time. On the 29th March, defendant came to plaintiff saying that he had a bill to pay, and he would be obliged if plaintiff would pay him £4O on account for the oats. Plaintiff at first demurred, but finally consented, and £4O was paid on April Ist. On April 11, having occasi•oi to require some of his pur- chase, plaintiff sent John Taylor for some. When this person entered the granary, he tound the grain to be heated, musty, and unmerchantable, and it would only pass as damaged oats, Mr. Edwards immediately can- celled the agreement. The action now was to recover the £4O paid down, and also an additional £lO as the price of corn had risen since the bargain was struck. Plaintiff and John Taylor each gave evidence in support of the counsel's statement.—Mr. Swift contended that his client was not responsible for the damage as plain- tiff had seen the oats before he made the bargain with Brereton. If a man were allowed to draw out a bar- gain which he had made, when he became dissatisfied with it, tbere would be no security in a bargain at all. In this case a specific bargain ha4l been struck, there- fore plaintiff was bound to it. Breroton on being called would not say that the oafs were bad even now. He had taken measures to prevent them heating. The judge briefly summed up, and the jury after an hour's consideration found damages for plaintiff for £4O.

BARBERS AND SUNDAY StfAVIVG.

At Blackburn, yesterday, four barhers, named Wall- hank, Parton, Heaton, and Monk, were summoned at the instance of the local Hairdresser's Union, under 29 Charles 11., cao. 7, for exercising their ordinary vocation on the Lord's Day.—Mr. Carter, wise prosecuted, said a strong sentiment doubtless existed against such prosecu- tions, but the law existed, and that was sufficient.— Evidence was given by Frederick Wood, vice-president of the union, of the committal of the offence. Witness admitted that only half the hairdressers in the town were members of the union, and that the half who were members were going to prosecate the other half. They had men watching all the shops on Sunday last.—The Chairman said he was sorry to sorry to say the bench avert equally divided, and they therefore dismissed the case. They were willing to state a case for appeal if require.%

GOLBO'RNE. CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH.—The anniversary services in connection with the above place of worship were held on Sunday. In the morning there was a children's ser- vice, appropriate hymns being sung by the children and an address given by Mr. Alfred Hulme, of St. Helens. The Rev. W. Karfont, M.A., L.L.D., of Leigh, preached to large congregations in the afternoon and evening, the church being crowded at the latter service. The choir rendered special music at both services, Mr. Walter Jones, organist and choirmaster, presiding at the organ. The collections realised the satisfactory amount of £3O Os. Id. CONGREGATIONAL GUILD.—The first of a series Of "At Homes" in connection with the above Guild was held on Saturday evening in the schoolroom, and was well at- tended, over 100 persons toning present. The Rev. T. Camm, pastor, received the guests, after which Mr. Granville Hunt presided, supported by the nastor and Mr. W. Sandham (superintendent). An interesting pro- gramme of recitations, glees,'and songs, were given, in- terspersed by short ad dresses ; one of the songs,. " The Vacant Chair," tieing sung in memory of the late James 1111lid,iv, one of the first members of the guild. who died about a fortnight ago after a lingering illness.
